An Approximation Theory for Metric Space-Valued Functions With A View Towards Deep Learning
Anastasis Kratsios, Chong Liu, Matti Lassas, Maarten V. de Hoop, Ivan Dokmanić

Abstract
Motivated by the developing mathematics of deep learning, we build universal functions approximators of continuous maps between arbitrary Polish metric spaces 𝒳 \mathcal{X} and 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} using elementary functions between Euclidean spaces as building blocks. Earlier results assume that the target space 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} is a topological vector space. We overcome this limitation by “randomization”: our approximators output discrete probability measures over 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} . When 𝒳 \mathcal{X} and 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} are Polish without additional structure, we prove very general qualitative guarantees; when they have suitable combinatorial structure, we prove quantitative guarantees for Hölder-like maps, including maps between finite graphs, solution operators to rough differential equations between certain Carnot groups, and continuous non-linear operators between Banach spaces arising in inverse problems. In particular, we show that the required number of Dirac measures is determined by the combinatorial structure of 𝒳 \mathcal{X} and 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} . For barycentric 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} , including Banach spaces, ℝ \mathbb{R} -trees, Hadamard manifolds, or Wasserstein spaces on Polish metric spaces, our approximators reduce to 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} -valued functions. When the Euclidean approximators are neural networks, our constructions generalize transformer networks, providing a new probabilistic viewpoint of geometric deep learning.



Keywords
: Approximation Theory, Rough Paths, Inverse Problems, Metric Spaces.

MSC (2022)
: 41A65, 68T07, 60L50, 65N21, 46T99.

Introduction

Universal approximation theorems are guarantees that classes of functions with favourable algorithmic properties, such as those that can be efficiently computed on modern hardware, are dense within given function spaces. Universal approximation was pioneered in neural network theory in the early
90
s by Hornik and Cybenko
[
1
,
2
]
, who showed that for any compact
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
, non-polynomial
1
1

1

The set of continuous functions from a topological space
\mathcal{X}
to a metric space
\mathcal{Y}
is denoted by
C(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
. Unless otherwise stated, this set is equipped with the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets, i.e. the compact-open topology.

\sigma\in C(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
, any
f\in C(K,\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and any
\varepsilon>0
, there is an integer
k\in\mathbb{N}
, a vector
b\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and matrices
A\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times m}
and
C\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
, such that

\sup_{x\in K}~\|f(x)-C(\sigma\circ(Ax+b))\|<\varepsilon.

(1.1)

This motivated that the class of functions

\mathcal{F}=\left\{x\mapsto C(\sigma\circ(Ax+b)):\,A\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times m},\,b\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\,C\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}\right\},

(1.2)

known in machine learning as single-hidden-layer neural networks, be called a
universal approximator
for
C(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{n})
. There exist many other universal approximators for
C(\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{n})
; classical examples preceding neural networks include polynomials
[
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
]
and splines
[
7
,
8
,
9
]
.

The universal approximation theory of neural networks has since seen a spate of results focusing on the advantages of multiple hidden layers (the so-called network
depth
) and sharpening guarantees for functions with various regularity priors. While there has been limited work on approximation between Riemannian manifolds
[
10
,
11
,
12
]
and between function spaces,
[
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
,
18
]
, most results address finite-dimensional normed source and target spaces and focus on cases where approximation rates can be improved either by exploiting assumptions on the target function regularity
[
19
,
20
,
21
,
22
,
23
]
or by fine-tuning the structure of the usual feed-forward network (cf. (
1.2
))
[
24
,
25
,
26
,
27
]
. In this paper, we propose a significant generalization of these results.

When
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are more general than finite-dimensional normed spaces, a key difficulty in building a universal class for
C(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
lies between topology and efficient computation. Whenever
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are non-contractible,
C(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
has multiple (often infinitely many) connected components
[
28
]
. A class
\mathcal{F}
can only be a universal approximator for
C(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
if it contains universal approximators for each connected component of
C(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
. Since the connected components are enumerated by the homotopy classes in
C(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
, universal approximation by
\mathcal{F}
demands that it exhausts all homotopy classes. A direct verification of universality within each class would require enumerating the homotopy classes. This, in turn requires computing the homotopy groups of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, which is in general NP-hard even when we are given simplicial complexes for
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, and even when they are as topologically simple as spheres
[
29
,
30
,
31
]
.

We sidestep this topological obstruction by randomizing the approximation problem
2
2

2

Randomization has often been a powerful tool to deal with problems that are “hard to crack” deterministically. Examples include Kantorovich’s randomization of transport maps in optimal transportation
[
32
]
as opposed to Monge’s deterministic construction, which need not exist, Shannon’s communication over noisy channels with an arbitrarily low but non-zero probability of error
[
33
,
34
]
as opposed to earlier attempts at errorless communication, and randomized algorithms which achieve polynomial time and space complexity as opposed to their computationally hard deterministic counterparts
[
35
]
.
. Instead of approximating a function
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
, we lift the problem to probability measures by approximating the map
\mathcal{X}\ni x\mapsto\delta_{f(x)}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})
taking values in the 1-Wasserstein space of probability measures on
\mathcal{Y}
. This construction can be seen as a far-reaching generalization of the standard practice in binary pattern classification where instead of directly approximating a classifier
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\{0,1\}
(with
0
and
1
labelling the two “classes”), one replaces
f
with a Markov kernel mapping
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to the set of probability measures on
\{0,1\}
3
3

3

This should be contrasted with approaches which randomize parameters of approximators
[
36
]
.
. Since
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})
is contractible,
C(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y}))
has a single homotopy class, and thus all the above topological challenges vanish.

Passing to probability measures allows us to construct
randomized
universal approximators of maps
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
between
arbitrary Polish metric spaces

\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, thus greatly extending the reach of comparably general theorems from approximation theory
[
37
,
38
,
39
,
40
,
41
,
42
,
11
,
16
,
43
,
44
]
.
When
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are Polish metric spaces, without additional structure, we prove a very general qualitative guarantee which can be informally summarized as follows.

Result A

(Theorem
3.3
, informal statement)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})
and
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
be Polish metric spaces,
\varphi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow F
a continuous and injective “feature map” into a suitable Banach “feature space”
F
, and suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is a family of maps which universally approximates continuous functions between Euclidean spaces, uniformly on compact sets.
Then for any continuous map
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
and
\varepsilon>0
and any compact
K\subseteq\mathcal{X}
there exists a function
\hat{T}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})
(written down explicitly in (
3.1
)-(
3.2
)) which satisfies

\sup_{x\in K}\,\mathcal{W}_{1}(\hat{T}(x),\delta_{f(x)})<\varepsilon.

\mathcal{F}

\mathbb{R}^{n}
to some

N

\mu_{n}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\sum_{q=1}^{Q}\,[P_{\Delta_{Q}}(u^{n})]_{q}\,\delta_{y^{n}_{{}_{\lceil z^{n}_{q}\rceil}}}
for

.
\mu_{1}
,

(light green) in

.

Result
A
implies
6
6

6

See Corollary
2.10
for a precise statement.
that any for any finite set
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\}
of inputs in
\mathcal{X}
, we can generate
\mathcal{Y}
–valued random variables
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{N}
with distributions
\hat{T}(x_{1}),\ldots,\hat{T}(x_{N})
which probabilistically approximate
f
in the usual sense; i.e.,

\mathbb{P}\biggl(\max_{n=1,\ldots,N}\,d_{\mathcal{Y}}(Y_{n},f(x_{n}))\leq\varepsilon\biggr)\geq 1-\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon).

Thus, topological obstructions to universal approximation imposed by
\mathcal{Y}
can be removed by sampling the measure-valued approximator
\hat{T}
, and this procedure is universal with arbitrarily high probability.

Beyond these topological aspects, our construction immediately yields results for approximation of operators between Banach spaces, generalizing many of the recent results on learning solution operators in forward and inverse problems for partial differential equations (Section
4.3.2
), with favourable rates when these operators exhibit Hölder-like regularity. More generally, for barycentric
\mathcal{Y}
we can “de-randomize” the approximators by passing their output through a barycenter map
\beta:\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
, resulting in a map from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{Y}
. This is a special case of our main quantitative approximation result:

Result B

(Theorem
3.7
, informal statement)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{Y}
,
\varphi:\mathcal{X}\to F
and
\mathcal{F}
be as before, and in addition assume that
\mathcal{Y}
is barycentric with a barycenter mapping
\beta:\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
. Let
\hat{T}
be the mapping constructed in Result
A
. Then
\hat{t}:=\beta\circ\hat{T}
satisfies

\sup_{x\in K}\,d_{\mathcal{Y}}(\hat{t}(x),f(x))<\varepsilon.

\beta
(light blue arrows) on
\mathcal{Y}

f(x)
(red point).

The approximators constructed using Result
C
extend this construction in two ways: they implement several simultaneous local versions of this construction and relax the parameterized barycenter to a parameterized geodesic selection between the green points in
\mathcal{Y}
.

Finally, when
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
is a metric measure space,
\mathcal{Y}
admits combinatorial decompositions into barycentric parts,
f
is a Hölder-like

Functions of Hölder-like regularity extend the class of Hölder maps to include uniformly continuous functions of sub-polynomial regularity at small distances.
functions which does not transport too much of

’s mass to the interphase of each of

. The constructed approximator encodes both spaces’ geometries and it outputs a finitely supported probability measure whose number of atoms equals to the number of parts in
\mathcal{Y}
; furthermore, on a set of arbitrarily high probability, the approximator can be derandomized to a
\mathcal{Y}
-valued function, extending Result
B
to general geometries.

Result C

(Theorem
3.11
, informal statement)
.

Let

is decomposed into ‘‘barycentric parts’’

and

Then for any compactly supported Borel probability measure

Applications

We demonstrate the generality of these results by applying them to four approximation problems from various domains for which no similar approximation theory has been available:

•

Maps between finite (discrete) metric spaces often arising in computer science, such as graph colouring problems
[
45
,
46
]
, link prediction in network analysis
[
47
,
48
]
, and node classification
[
49
]
;

•

Maps between smooth manifolds
[
50
]
arising in shape-space analysis
[
51
]
or in statistics when approximating the inverse operator of acting on high-dimensional non-singular covariance matrices
[
52
]
with affine-invariant metric
[
53
]
;

•

Solution operators to rough differential equations which are defined on the non-smooth (sub-Riemannian) geometry of a particular Carnot group
[
54
,
55
]
;

•

Solution operators to inverse problems between infinite-dimensional Banach spaces
[
56
]
and their regularizations when reconstructions lie on finite-dimensional sub-manifolds
[
57
]
.

Our constructions are modular in the sense that they can be built around any given class of universal function approximators between Euclidean spaces, including deep neural networks
[
58
,
59
,
60
]
, kernel regressors
[
61
,
36
]
, regression trees
[
62
]
, and splines
[
63
]
. Thus, in particular, we contribute to the emerging mathematical theory of deep learning
[
64
,
19
]
.

Outline

We introduce the required notation and terminology in Section
2
. The main results are presented in Section
3
. In Section
4
, we collect diverse applications of our theoretical results to the approximation of maps arising in rough differential equations, computational geometry, graph theory, and in inverse problems. Section
5
contains the proofs of Theorem
3.3
, Theorem
3.7
and Theorem
3.11
from Section
3
.

2
Definitions and Background

We begin by reviewing the terminology and analytic tools:

•

In Section
2.1
we recall the definitions of doubling spaces and quasisymmetric maps. We also introduce a class of uniformly continuous functions which mimic the geometric properties of Hölder functions but allow for much lower regularity;

•

In Section
2.2
we give the background on quantizable and approximately simplicial spaces
[
44
]
, a class of spaces which admit sequences of progressively finer local finite-dimensional parameterizations. These spaces are conceptually similar to multi-resolution analysis on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

[
65
]
or quantization of probability measures in
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

[
66
]
;

•

In Section
2.3
we formalize the definition of classical universal approximators between linear spaces which are a building block of our randomized approximators.

2.1
Analysis on Metric Spaces and Quasi Symmetry

A metric space
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})
is said to be (
d_{\mathcal{X}}
-)
doubling
, with doubling constant
C_{\mathcal{X}}>0
, if
C_{\mathcal{X}}
is the smallest number such that for every
x\in\mathcal{X}
and every
r>0
, the metric ball
B_{\mathcal{X}}(x,2r)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\{u\in\mathcal{X}:\,d_{\mathcal{X}}(u,x)<2r\}
can be covered by
C_{\mathcal{X}}
metric balls of radius
r
in
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})
, and
C_{\mathcal{X}}<\infty
.
This implies that a doubling subset
K\subseteq\mathcal{X}
cannot be too large, in the sense that it can be deformed into a subset of a Euclidean space with only a small perturbation to its geometry
[
67
, Theorem 12.1]
. (We note in the passing that
some
perturbation is in generally necessary
[
68
,
69
]
.)

Quasisymmetric
maps are a class of maps which preserve the doubling property; they generalize conformal maps from complex analysis
[
70
]
. These maps preserve the geometry of a space by encoding the relative distance in triplets of points
9
9

9

See Lemma
5.12
for a quantitative estimate of a doubling space’s doubling constant under a quasisymmetric map.
. Let
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}[0,\infty)
.
A topological embedding
\varphi:(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})\rightarrow(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
is said to be quasisymmetric if there is a strictly increasing surjective map
\eta_{\varphi}:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, satisfying

d_{\mathcal{X}}(x_{1},x_{2})\leq td_{\mathcal{X}}(x_{1},x_{3})\mbox{ implies that }d_{\mathcal{Y}}(\varphi(x_{1}),\varphi(x_{2}))\leq\eta_{\varphi}(t)d_{\mathcal{Y}}(\varphi(x_{1}),\varphi(x_{3}))

for all
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in\mathcal{X}
and
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
.

In this paper, we study continuous functions
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
which exhibit a
Hölder-like
regularity.

Definition 2.1

(Hölder-Like Functions)
.

A uniformly continuous map
f:(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})\rightarrow(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
which admits a strictly increasing, subadditive and continuous modulus of continuity
\omega
for which there is an increasing homeomorphism
h_{\omega}:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
, satisfying

\omega(st)\leq h_{\omega}(s)\,\omega(t)

for every
s,t\geq 0
, is said to be
\omega
-Hölder-like;
\omega
is the corresponding Hölder-like modulus of continuity.

When clear from the context, we omit the explicit dependence on
\omega
and say
f
is Hölder-like.

Example 2.2

(Hölder Functions are of Hölder-Like Regularity)
.

Fix
L\geq 0
and
0<\alpha\leq 1,0\leq\beta\leq 1-\alpha
. Then
\omega(t)=Lt^{\alpha}(\log(1+t))^{\beta}
is concave
10
10

10

Acknowledgement: Andrew Colinet, who also noted the correct constraint on
\beta
to ensure concavity of this modulus of continuity.
, implying the subadditivity of
\omega(\cdot)
. Thus
\omega(t)
is a Hölder-like modulus of continuity with
h_{\omega}(s)=s^{\alpha}\max\{1,s\}^{\beta}
, since
h_{\omega}(s)=s^{\alpha}\sup_{u>0}\frac{\log(1+(us))^{\beta}}{\log(1+u)^{\beta}}
and
\sup_{u>0}\frac{\log(1+(us))^{\beta}}{\log(1+u)^{\beta}}=1
when
0\leq s\leq 1
and equals
s^{\beta}
otherwise.
∎

Hölder-like maps need not have regularity similar to any Hölder function near
0
, which is the region impacting quantitative approximation rates. The next class of examples shows that the class of Hölder-like functions contain maps whose modulus of continuity converges to
0
slower than any Hölder function.

Example 2.3

(Hölder-Like Moduli of Sub-Hölder Regularity
11
11

11

Acknowledgement: Andrew Colinet.
)
.

Fix a parameter
\beta>0
. An example of a Hölder-like modulus of continuity
\omega
with the property that
\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow 0^{+}}\,t^{\alpha}\omega^{-1}(t)=0
for every
0<\alpha\leq 1
is

\omega(t)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\begin{cases}0&\mbox{ if }t=0\\
\frac{1}{|\log(t)|^{\beta}}&\mbox{ if }0<t\leq\frac{1}{e^{\beta+1}}\\
\frac{1}{(\beta+1)^{\beta}}+\frac{\beta e^{\beta+1}}{(\beta+1)^{\beta+1}}(t-\frac{1}{e^{\beta+1}})&\mbox{ if }\frac{1}{e^{\beta+1}}<t<\infty.\end{cases}

(2.1)

The function
\omega
is increasing, concave (therefore sub-additive), continuous with homeomorphism
h_{\omega}(s)=\max\{s^{\beta},s\}
satisfying
\sup_{u>0}\,\frac{\omega(us)}{\omega(u)}\leq h_{\omega}(s)\leq s^{\beta}
for
0\leq s\leq 1
and
\sup_{u>0}\,\frac{\omega(us)}{\omega(u)}\leq h_{\omega}(s)\leq s
otherwise.
∎

Hölder-like maps become Lipschitz under perturbations of the geometry of the source space
\mathcal{X}
known as the generalized snowflake
12
12

12

The terminology “snowflake” stems from the observation that
(\mathbb{R},|\cdot|^{\alpha})
with
\alpha=\frac{\log(3)}{\log(4)}
is isometric to the Koch snowflake with metric inherited from inclusion in the Euclidean plane.
transform
[
71
]
.
Since this perturbation is a quasi-symmetry, it preserves the doubling property of the source space.

Lemma 2.4

(Generalized Snowflakes are Quasisymmetric to their Original Space)
.

Let
\omega
be a Hölder-like modulus and
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})
a metric space. Then,
d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\omega\circ d_{\mathcal{X}}
defines a metric on
\mathcal{X}
and
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})\ni x\mapsto x\in(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega})
is a quasi-symmetry.
Furthermore, if
K\subseteq\mathcal{X}
is
d_{\mathcal{X}}
-doubling then it is also
d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}
-doubling.

Remark 2.5
.

The doubling constant with respect to
d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}
can be explicitly computed; see Lemma
5.2
.

Lemma
2.4
implies that approximating Hölder-like maps is equivalent to approximating Lipschitz maps with a judiciously perturbed metric. Note however that in general one cannot expect to approximate continuous functions by Hölder functions; for example, no map from
(\mathbb{R},|\cdot|)
to
(\mathbb{R},\log(1+|\cdot|))
is Hölder continuous.

The generalized snowflake transform perturbs the geometry of
\mathcal{X}
enough to make all Hölder functions Lipschitz. By further distorting
\mathcal{X}
’s metric while preserving its topology, we can approximate all continuous maps from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{Y}
by functions which are Lipschitz for the perturbed metric. As a consequence, quantitative statements about approximation of Lipschitz functions between Polish metric spaces imply qualitative statements about general continuous functions.

Lemma 2.6

(Most Continuous Functions are Lipschitz
13
13

13

Acknowledgment: Joseph Van Name.
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a compact Polish space, and let
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
be a separable metric space. There is a metric
d_{\mathcal{X}}
on
\mathcal{X}
which generates
\mathcal{X}
’s topology such that the set of Lipschitz maps from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{Y}
is uniformly dense in the set
C(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
of continuous functions from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{Y}
.

We are interested in uniformly approximating continuous maps on compact subsets of the source metric space. The lemmata above imply that it is sufficient to focus on the approximation theory of Lipschitz maps.

2.2
Quantizable and Approximately Simplicial (QAS) Spaces

Quantizable and approximately simplicial spaces (QAS) spaces are a class of metric spaces
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
which capture some of the essential metric properties of Wasserstein spaces, from optimal transport theory, as they pertain to approximation theory
[
44
]
, which like the Wasserstein spaces over any polish spaces are asymptotically parametrizable by Euclidean vectors. The construction can also be interpreted as a non-Euclidean analogue of multi-resolution analysis on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
used in signal processing
[
65
]
, where one progressively refines their description of a set of functions by using more progressively frequencies.
As we will see in our applications section, most reasonable spaces are QAS spaces.

The
N
-simplex, which parameterizes any the space of probability measures on
N
points is denoted by

\Delta_{N}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\{w\in[0,1]^{N}:\,\sum_{n=1}^{N}w_{n}=1\}
. For any given metric space
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
define

\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\bigcup_{N\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}\left(\Delta_{N}\times\mathcal{Y}^{N}\right).

Using
\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}
, we can approximately parameterize
\mathcal{Y}
by inscribing simplices in
\mathcal{Y}
.

Definition 2.7

(Approximately Simplicial)
.

A metric space
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
is said to be approximately simplicial if there is
a function
\eta:\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}\to\mathcal{Y}
, called a mixing function, and constants
C_{\eta}\geq 1
and
p\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, such that
for every
N\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
w=(w_{1},\dots,w_{N})\in\Delta_{N}
and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{N})\in\mathcal{Y}^{N}
, and for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
one has

d_{\mathcal{Y}}\left(\eta\left(w,\mathbf{y}\right),y_{i}\right)\leq C_{\eta}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}d_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{i},y_{j})^{p}w_{j}\right)^{1/p}.

(2.2)

In particular, the function
\eta
satisfies
\eta(e_{i},\mathbf{y})=y_{i},i=1,\dots,N
, where
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
is the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
.

Intuitively, the mixing function
\eta
mixes finite sets of points in
\mathcal{Y}
by moving along geodesic segments and this mixing is parameterized by Euclidean simplices.
Quantizability can roughly be understood as a simultaneously quantitative and “asymptotically parametric” analogue of the otherwise qualitative separability property of a topological space
[
44
]
.

Definition 2.8

(Quantization)
.

Let
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
be a metric space and let
Q_{\cdot}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}(Q_{q})_{q\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
be a family of functions
Q_{q}:{\mathbb{R}}^{D_{q}}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
with each
D_{q}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
satisfying

1.

for all
q\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and each
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D_{q}}
, there exists some
\tilde{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D_{q+1}}
with

Q_{q}(z)=Q_{q+1}(\tilde{z});

2.

for every
y\in\mathcal{Y}
and each
\varepsilon>0
, there exists some
q\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and some
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D_{q}}
such that

d_{\mathcal{Y}}\left(y,Q_{q}(z)\right)<\varepsilon.

The family
Q
is a quantization of
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
if for any compact subset
K
of
\mathcal{Y}
and each
\varepsilon>0
the quantization modulus

\mathscr{Q}_{K}(\varepsilon)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\inf\left\{D_{q}:\,(\forall y\in K)\,\exists z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{D_{q}}\mbox{ such that }d_{\mathcal{Y}}(y,Q_{q}(z))<\varepsilon\right\}

is finite. The map
\mathscr{Q}_{K}
is called the modulus of quantizability of
K
.

If
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
is quantizable by
Q_{\cdot}=(Q_{q})_{q\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
and approximately simplicial with mixing function
\eta
, then we can approximately implement any generalized geodesic simplex in
\mathcal{Y}
by combining these two structures via

\displaystyle\hat{\eta}:\bigcup_{N,q\in\mathbb{N}_{+},N\geq 2}\,\Delta_{N}\times\mathbb{R}^{N\times D_{q}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}

(2.3)

\displaystyle\hat{\eta}(w,Z)

\displaystyle\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\eta(w,(Q_{q}(Z_{1}),\dots,Q_{q}(Z_{N}))),

where
w\in\Delta_{N}
,
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{D_{q}}
and
N,D_{q}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
. The map
\hat{\eta}
, which can be interpreted as a quantized version of the mixing function
\eta
, is called the
quantized mixing map
. A triple
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}},\hat{\eta})
is called a
QAS space
if
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
is
q
uantizable and
a
pproximately
s
implicial with
\hat{\eta}
defined by (
2.3
).

A Prototypical Example: (First) Wasserstein Space over Separable Metric Spaces

We fix ideas with a prototypical QAS space, namely, the (first) Wasserstein space
(\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{X}),W_{1})
above a separable metric space
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})
, a metric space which plays a central role throughout our analysis. The elements of
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{X})
are probability measures
\mu
on
\mathcal{X}
with finite first moment, meaning that there is some
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
for which the integral
\int\,d_{\mathcal{X}}(x,x_{0})\mu(dx)
is finite. The distance between any two
\mu
and
\nu
therein measures the minimal amount of “work” required to move mass from
\mu
and
\nu
,

W_{1}(\mu,\nu)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\inf_{\pi}\,\int d(x_{1},x_{2})\,\pi(d(x_{1},x_{2})),

(2.4)

where the minimization is over all Radon probability distributions
\pi
on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
whose push-forwards by the canonical projections are
\mu
and
\nu
. We will repeatedly use the fact that
\mathcal{X}
isometrically embeds into
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{X})
via the map
x\mapsto\delta_{x}
, where
\delta_{x}
is the point mass on
x
(see
[
72
,
73
,
74
]
for details).

An example of a mixing function
\eta
on
(\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{X}),W_{1})
sends any weight
w
in an
N
-simplex
\Delta_{N}
and any set of
N
probability measures
\mu_{1}\dots,\mu_{N}
in
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{X})
to their convex combination,

\eta(w,(\mu_{n})_{n=1}^{N})=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\,w_{n}\,\mu_{n}.

(2.5)

It is easy to show that such
\eta
satisfies the inequality (
2.2
) with
C_{\eta}=1
and
p=1
.
Quantizability comes into play as probability measures in
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{X})
need not be exactly describable as mixtures, in the sense of
\eta
, of finitely many “elementary probability measures”. Since
\mathcal{X}
is separable, there is a countable dense subset
\{y_{q}\}_{q\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\mathcal{X}
, and by the proof of
[
75
, Theorem 6.18]
, the set of probability measures on
\mathcal{X}
supported on finite subsets of
\{y_{q}\}_{q\in\mathbb{N}}
is dense in
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{X})
. We can therefore define the quantization maps
\mathcal{Q}_{q}:\mathbb{R}^{2\times Q}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{X})
by

\mathcal{Q}_{q}(u,z)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\,[P_{\Delta_{q}}(u)]_{i}\,\,\delta_{y_{{}_{\lceil z_{i}\rceil}}},

where
v_{i}
denotes the
i^{th}
component of a vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
,
P_{\Delta_{q}}:\mathbb{R}^{q}\rightarrow\Delta_{q}
is the Euclidean orthogonal projection onto the
q
-simplex
\Delta_{q}
, and

y_{{}_{\lceil z_{i}\rceil}}=y_{n},
where
n=\lceil z_{i}\rceil\in\mathbb{Z}
smallest integer satisfying
n\geq z_{i}
.
Combining
\eta
and
\mathcal{Q}_{\cdot}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}(\mathcal{Q}_{q})_{q\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
, we obtain our quantized mixing function

\hat{\eta}\big(w,(u_{i,j},z_{i,j})_{i,j=1}^{I,q}\big)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\,w_{i}\,\cdot\,\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{q}\,[P_{\Delta_{q}}(u_{i,j})]_{j}\,\delta_{y_{{}_{\lceil z_{i,j}\rceil}}}\biggr)\,,

(2.6)

where
Z\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}(u_{i,j},z_{i,j})_{i,j=1}^{I,q}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times I\times q}
and where
\mathbb{R}^{2\times I\times q}
is identified with
\mathbb{R}^{2Iq}
.
The global geometry of
\mathcal{X}
is encoded in several ways in the Wasserstein space
(\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{X}),W_{1})
. A useful interpretation of QAS spaces arises from considering the metric analogue of the geometric realization of
\mathcal{X}
’s Vietoris–Rips complex. For a radius parameter
r>0
, the
r
-thick metric Vietoris–Rips complex
[
76
]
, denoted by
\operatorname{VR}_{r}^{\operatorname{m}}(\mathcal{X})
, is a metric space which is often homeomorphic to
\mathcal{X}
but can be easier to work with from the computational topology perspective
[
77
, Theorem 4.6]
. The connection with QAS spaces is from its realization of a metric subspace of
(\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{X}),W_{1})
defined as

\operatorname{VR}_{r}^{\operatorname{m}}(\mathcal{X})\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\left\{\sum_{i=0}^{k}\,w_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}:\,k\in\mathbb{N}_{+},\,w\in\Delta_{k},\,\max_{i,j\leq k}\,d_{\mathcal{X}}(x_{i},x_{j})\leq r\right\}.

(2.7)

Comparing (
2.6
) and (
2.7
), we notice that the image of the quantized mixing function subsumes
\operatorname{VR}_{r}^{\operatorname{m}}(\mathcal{X})
.
\operatorname{VR}_{r}^{\operatorname{m}}(\mathcal{X})
are formed by convex combinations of point masses in
\mathcal{X}
which are close enough (at a maximum distance of
r
). By comparsion, in
\hat{\eta}
’s image, we can take convex combinations of point masses
14
14

14

More precisely, these are instead supported on the dense subset
\{x_{q}\}_{q\in\mathbb{N}}
not on any of the possibly uncountable points in
\mathcal{X}
.
with the main difference being that
\eta
automatically adapts to the closeness of the points in
\mathcal{X}
as it satisfies the condition
15
15

15

See
[
44
, Example 8]
.
(
2.2
).

Another way in which
\mathcal{X}
’s global geometry is reflected in
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{X})
is through the
barycentricity
property, which can be understood as a far-reaching abstraction of the geometry of the
1
-Wasserstein space over a Banach space.
A metric space is barycentric if the isometric embedding
x\mapsto\delta_{x}
of
\mathcal{X}
in
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{X})
has a uniformly continuous right inverse
[
78
,
79
]
. This is because, as shown in
[
80
]
, any Banach space
\mathcal{X}
admits a unique Lipschitz right-inverse
\beta
to the map
x\mapsto\delta_{x}
exists and it is simply given by the Bochner integration. Furthermore, every barycentric metric space
\mathcal{X}
is approximately simplicial with mixing function

\eta(w,(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}))=\beta\circ\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}\biggl),

consequentially, every Polish barycentric metric spaces is a QAS space.
In this case one can choose
C_{\eta}=1
and
p=1
in the inequality (
2.2
), details are developed in Section
4.2
.

Barycentric metric spaces are precisely those that admit conical geodesic bicombings
[
81
]
; we explore bicombings further in applications of our approximation theory to rough differential equations in Section
4.2
. Barycentricity is a transport-theoretic analogue of a non-expansive barycenter map in non-linear Banach space theory
[
82
]
, which is defined similarly on a certain Banach space containing a copy
16
16

16

This Banach space contains an isometric image of
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{X})
; see Lemma
5.1
for details.
of the
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{X})
.
We note that for any compact set of probability measures
K
in
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{X})
the modulus of quantizability
\mathscr{Q}_{K}(\varepsilon)
is precisely the uniform rate of quantization; this is known in the case where
\mathcal{X}
is doubling and the measures in
K
satisfy certain moment conditions
[
83
]
. Optimal constants are known when the measures in
K
are compactly supported and have the same (finite) Assouad dimension
[
84
,
66
]
.

Randomized Approximation of Points in Metric Spaces

Just as the notion of closeness between two points in a metric space
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
may be extended to the distance between a non-empty compact subset of
\mathcal{Y}
and a point, we may give meaning to the distance between a probability measure
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})
and a point
y
in
\mathcal{Y}
. Figure
3
illustrates this idea by showing a sequence of probability measures which intuitively approach a point. Using the isometric embedding of
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
into
(\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y}),W_{1})
sending any point
y\in\mathcal{Y}
to the point-mass
\delta_{y}
, we define the distance between
\mathbb{P}
and
y
as

d_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbb{P},y)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}W_{1}(\mathbb{P},\delta_{y}),

noting that if
\mathbb{P}=\delta_{\tilde{y}}
for some point
\tilde{y}\in\mathcal{Y}
then
d_{\mathcal{Y}}(\delta_{\tilde{y}},y)
is precisely
d_{\mathcal{Y}}(\tilde{y},y)
. In this way,
d_{\mathcal{Y}}
is a map from
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
extending the metric
d_{\mathcal{Y}}
upon isometrically identifying
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
with
(\{\delta_{y}\}_{y\in\mathcal{Y}},W_{1})
.

(a)

Approximation by probability measure with mass spread over
\mathcal{Y}
.

(b)

Approximation by concentrated multi-modal probability measure.

(c)

Approximation by probability measure with concentrated mass.

Figure 3
:
Approximation of a point (light-blue) by probability measures with progressively higher concentration. The regions of high concentration of these measures are illustrated in dark blue and the regions of low-concentration are illustrated in white, regions of intermediate concentration are depicted in purple.

The
1
-Wasserstein distance to a point mass admits a simple expression reflecting Figure
3
.
17
17

17

We note that this is not the case for general empirical distributions: even when
\mathcal{Y}
is a Euclidean space of dimension
2
or more, fast algorithms to compute the Wasserstein-
1
distance between empirical distributions such as the accelerated primal-dual randomized coordinate descent
[
85
]
have super-quadratic complexity.
As a result we obtain a simple expression for the distance between
\mathcal{Y}
-valued and
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})
-valued functions.

Proposition 2.9

(Closed-Form Expression of Distance Between
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})
-Valued and
\mathcal{Y}
-Valued Functions)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
be metric spaces,
\hat{T}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})
, and
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
. Then for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
we have

\mathcal{W}_{1}\big(\hat{T}(x),\delta_{f(x)}\big)=\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim\hat{T}(x)}\big[d_{\mathcal{Y}}(Y,f(x))\big],

and, in particular, the suprema over
\mathcal{X}
of both sides coincide.

Proposition
2.9
implies that if
f
is approximated by a
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})
-valued function
\hat{T}
on finite subsets of the source space
\mathcal{X}
to
\varepsilon>0
precision, then with high probability, it can be uniformly approximated on that finite set by independent random variables whose laws are dictated by the approximator
\hat{T}
.

Corollary 2.10

(Approximation by
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})
-Valued Maps Imply High-Probability Estimates on Finite Sets)
.

Assume the setting of Proposition
2.9
and let
\varepsilon>0
.
If
\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\,\mathcal{W}_{1}\big(\hat{T}(x),\delta_{f(x)}\big)<\varepsilon
,
\mathcal{X}=\{x_{n}\}_{n=1}^{N}
for a positive integer
N
, and
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{N}
are independent random variables on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
with
Y_{n}
distributed according to
\hat{T}(x_{n})
, for
n=1,\dots,N
, then

\mathbb{P}\Biggl(\max_{n=1,\dots,N}\,d_{\mathcal{Y}}\big(Y_{n},f(x_{n})\big)\leq N\sqrt{\varepsilon}\Biggr)\geq\biggl(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{N}\biggr)^{N}.

2.3
Approximation in Linear Spaces

In the following, we let
\operatorname{Comp}(\mathcal{X})
denote the collection of compact subsets of a given metric space
\mathcal{X}
.

Definition 2.11

(Universal Approximator)
.

Consider a family
\mathcal{F}_{\cdot}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\{\mathcal{F}_{n,m,c}\}_{n,m,c=1}^{\infty}
, where each
\{\mathcal{F}_{n,m,c}\}_{c=1}^{\infty}
is a nested family of functions mapping
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
\mathbb{R}^{m}
together with a map
r:[C({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}}_{+})\times\operatorname{Comp}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\times\mathbb{N}_{+}^{2}]\times\mathbb{N}_{+}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. We call
\mathcal{F}_{\cdot}
a universal approximator with rate function
r
if
for any pair of positive integers
n,m
, any uniformly continuous
f:{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}^{m}}
with continuous modulus of continuity
\omega
, and any non-empty compact
K\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
it holds that

1.

for every
c>0
there is some
\hat{f}\in\mathcal{F}_{n,m,c}
satisfying the uniform estimate

\underset{x\in K}{\sup}\,\left\|f(x)-\hat{f}(x)\right\|\leq r(\omega,K,n,m,c);

2.

the function
c\mapsto r(\omega,K,m,c)
is decreasing with
\lim\limits_{c\to\infty}\,r(\omega,K,m,c)=0
.

Classical examples of universal approximators are Bernstein polynomials
[
3
,
4
,
86
,
5
,
6
,
87
]
, piecewise constant functions (for example trees)
[
88
,
89
]
, splines
[
7
,
8
,
90
,
9
,
91
]
, wavelets
[
92
,
93
,
94
,
63
]
. Examples of universal approximators central to contemporary approximation theory, computer science, and machine learning are feedforward
[
95
,
96
,
59
,
97
]
and convolutional neural networks
[
58
,
98
]
. A typical example is the class of piecewise linear functions known as ReLU networks (i.e. neural networks which activation functions are Rectified Linear Units).

Example 2.12

(ReLU Networks)
.

The set
\mathcal{F}_{n,m,c}
of piecewise linear maps
\hat{f}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
with representation

\displaystyle\hat{f}(x)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}A^{(c+1)}x^{(c)}+b^{(c)}\,;\qquad x^{(t+1)}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\operatorname{ReLU}\big(A^{(t)}x^{(t)}+b^{(t)}\big)\,\,(t=0,\dots,c);\qquad x^{(0)}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}x.

where each
A^{(t)}
is a
d_{t+1}\times d_{t}
matrix,
b^{(t)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{t+1}}
, the
\operatorname{ReLU}
function acts on vectors
x\in\cup_{d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}\,\mathbb{R}^{d}
by
\operatorname{ReLU}((x_{i})_{i=1}^{d})\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}(\max\{x_{i},0\})_{i=1}^{d}
,
d_{0}=n
,
d_{c+1}=m
, and
d_{t}\leq c
for every
t=1,\dots,c
, is such that
\mathcal{F}_{\cdot}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\{\mathcal{F}_{n,m,c}\}_{n,m,c\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
is a universal approximator.
Its rate function
r
is given in
[
99
, Theorem 1]
.
∎

Our next tool does not concern the approximation of functions but of linear spaces themselves.
We recall the definition of the
bounded approximation property
(BAP) introduced in
[
100
]
. Given a constant
C>0
, a Banach space
F
has the
C
-
Bounded Approximation Property
(
C
-BAP) if
18
18

18

An alternative characterization of the
C
-BAP formulation in terms of stable
\sqrt{C}
-manifold widths, as defined on
[
101
, page 612]
, can be found in
[
101
, Theorem 2.4]
.
for every non-empty compact subset
K\subseteq F
there are finite-rank operators
\{T_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
on
F
satisfying

\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\,\max_{x\in K}\,\left\|T_{n}(x)-x\right\|=0\,\mbox{and}\,\|T_{n}\|_{op}\leq C~\text{for all}~n\in\mathbb{N};

(2.8)

where
\|\cdot\|_{op}
is the operator norm of
T
.
We say that
T_{\cdot}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}(T_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
realize the
C
-BAP on
K
if (
2.8
) holds. We also say that
F
has the BAP if it has the
C
-BAP for some
C>0
.
We quantify the rate at which
(T_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
approximates the identity using the map
19
19

19

The map
R^{T_{\cdot}:K}
is, by definition, lower-bounded by the linear width of the compact set
K
(see
[
102
]
for details) and, a fortiori, by various non-linear widths of
K
such as its Kolmogorov width
[
103
]
or its Lipschitz width
[
104
]
.

R^{T_{\cdot}:K}:(0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{+}
defined for every
\varepsilon>0
by

R^{T_{\cdot}:K}(\varepsilon)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\min\big\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}:\,\max_{x\in K}\,\left\|T_{n}(x)-x\right\|\leq\varepsilon\big\}.

(2.9)

Generalized Inverses

Given a monotone increasing function
f:{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, we define its generalized inverse by
[
105
]

f^{\dagger}(t)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\inf\{s\in\mathbb{R}:\,f(s)\geq t\},

with the convention that the infimum of
\emptyset
is
\infty
.
If
f
is strictly increasing and surjective then
f^{\dagger}=f^{-1}
.

Norms Induced by Finite-Rank Operators

Let
T
be a finite-rank operator on
F
and let
\{e_{n}\}_{n=1}^{N}
be a basis for
T(F)
. Let
\iota_{T}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\ni x\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,x_{i}\cdot e_{i}\in F
. We define the norm
\|\cdot\|_{F:n}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
as

\|x\|_{F:n}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\|\iota_{T}(x)\|_{F}.

(2.10)

In words,
\|\cdot\|_{F:n}
is the pullback of the restriction
\|\cdot\|_{F}
to the finite-dimensional subspace spanned by the vectors in
T
’s image.

Typical Compact Sets

Given a metric space
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})
, one may measure the “distance” between non-empty closed subsets
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
of
\mathcal{X}
is using the
Hausdorff pseudo-metric

d_{\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})}
, defined by

d_{\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})}(K_{1},K_{2})\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\max\biggl\{\sup_{x\in K_{2}}d(x,K_{1}),\,\sup_{\tilde{x}\in K_{1}}d(K_{2},\tilde{x})\biggr\}.

The Hausdorff pseudo-metric defines a metric when restricted to the class of non-empty compact subsets of
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})
. We will call a family of (non-empty) compact subsets
\mathcal{K}
of a metric space
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})

typical
, if for every
\varepsilon>0
and every non-empty compact subset
K\subseteq\mathcal{X}
there is some
K_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{K}
satisfying the estimate:

d_{\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})}(K,K_{\varepsilon})<\varepsilon.

Equivalently,
\mathcal{K}
is typical precisely if and only if it is dense in the space of non-empty compact subsets of
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})
metrized by the Hausdorff metric.

3
Main Results

We begin by presenting our main qualitative result which constructs a class of randomized maps that universally approximate arbitrarily complex continuous functions between most metric spaces. In practice we expect that the parameter complexity of the approximant correlates with the complexity of the function being approximated.

We then complement this very general result by studying the case where the source and target space possess additional combinatorial structure. This allows us to refine the analysis by building the metric geometry of source and target spaces into the approximator. We obtain efficient quantitative approximation rates for
\omega
-Hölder-like functions which respect the said combinatorial structure. This result covers most spaces relevant to deep learning, operator learning, and learning on graphs.

3.1
General Case: Randomized Approximation

We summarize the structural assumptions about
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{Y}
, and the
feature map

\varphi
, which we assume exists, mapping
\mathcal{X}
into a suitable Banach space
F
, called a
feature space
.

Setting 3.1

(Qualitative Setting)
.

.

(i)

(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})
and
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
are Polish metric spaces;

(ii)

\varphi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow F
is a continuous injective map into a Banach space
F
with the BAP
20
20

20

One could weaken the sequence of finite-rank

(T^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}

Setting

Feature maps are typically constructed on a case-by-case basis. The next proposition establishes that a feature map satisfying Setting

Proposition 3.2

(Existence of Feature Maps into A Separable Hilbert Space)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})
be a Polish metric space. There exists a continuous injective map
\varphi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow(\ell^{2},\|\cdot\|_{2})
.

We are now ready to state our first main result which guarantees that arbitrary continuous functions between arbitrary Polish metric spaces can be approximated on compact sets by “randomized functions”.

Theorem 3.3

\mathcal{X}
and

(3.1)

realize the BAP on
\varphi(K)
, an approximator

with representation

(3.2)

which satisfies the estimate,

\sup_{x\in K}\,W_{1}\big(\hat{T}(x),\delta_{f(x)}\big)<\varepsilon.

Furthermore, there is a typical

Relationship to Transformer Networks

Introduced by
[
117
]
, transformer networks are a class of deep neural networks built using the attention mechanism of
[
118
]
, (a deep learning layer build suing softmax function, discussed below). Suppose that
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathcal{Y}=\mathbb{R}^{D}
,
\varphi=1_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, and
\mathcal{F}
is the set of deep feedforward networks in Example
2.12
. Note that
T_{n}=1_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
implements the BAP of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Taking the “expectation” of a random variable distributed according to each
\hat{T}(x)
in (
3.2
), in the sense of Bochner integration, yields a vector-valued function
\hat{t}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{D}
given by

\hat{t}(x)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\hat{T}(x)}[X]=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\,[P_{\Delta_{N}}(\hat{f}(x))]_{n}\,V_{n},

for each
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
V
is the
N\times d
matrix with rows
V_{n}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\biggl(\sum_{q=1}^{Q}\,[P_{\Delta_{Q}}(u^{n})]_{q}\,y^{n}_{\lceil z^{n}_{q}\rceil}\biggr)
.
The projection onto the
N
-simplex is analogous to the softmax function
23
23

23

Most of our analysis goes through with
P_{\Delta_{N}}
replaced by the softmax. A caveat is that it requires to handle the boundary of
\Delta_{N}
as a
\mathcal{Z}
-set, in the sense of
[
119
, Section 5]
, as in Theorem
[
11
, Theorem 37 and Example 13]
.

\operatorname{Softmax}_{N}:x\mapsto\Biggl(\frac{e^{x_{n}}}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\,e^{x_{i}}}\Biggr)_{n=1}^{N}.

If we replace
P_{\Delta_{N}}
with the
\operatorname{Softmax}_{N}
then
\hat{t}
becomes

\hat{t}\approx\sum_{n=1}^{N}\,[\operatorname{Softmax}_{N}(\hat{f}(\cdot))]_{n}\,V_{n}.

(3.3)

The map
u\mapsto\sum_{n=1}^{N}\,[\operatorname{Softmax}_{N}(u)]_{n}\,V_{n}
is simply the the attention layer of
[
118
]
, which is the main novel building block of transformer networks of
[
117
]
, and the matrix
V
is the matrix of ‘‘values’’
24
24

24

Typically one considers a more complicated attention layer which computes
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\,[\operatorname{Softmax}_{N}(K^{\top}Q)]_{n}V_{n}
from three sources: a matrix of “keys”
K
, a matrix of “queries”
Q
, and a matrix of “values”
V
. Like most mathematical analyses of deep learning models, e.g.
[
58
,
98
,
120
]
, (
3.3
) considers a mathematically tractable simplification of the transformer network where the matrix of keys

is always the
1\times 1
matrix
K=(1)
, and we identify the softmax function’s source
u\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
with the
1\times N
matrix
(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
; thus, for us
u=K^{\top}Q
. A similar simplification was assumed in the probabilistic transformer networks of
[
121
,
44
,
122
]
when approximating regular conditional distributions.
and the right-hand side of (
3.3
) is a simple instance of a transformer network.

3.2
Quantitative Universal Approximation

We now turn to the cases where
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
admit additional geometric structure.
We will first assume that
\mathcal{Y}
is a barycentric QAS space which will allow us to construct non-randomized function approximators.

The Structure of Source Spaces

To ensure that inputs in a general metric space
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})
are compatible with the Euclidean building block, we need to relate and compress information in
\mathcal{X}
into Euclidean data. We therefore require some structure of
\mathcal{X}
, akin to that of a (Banach) manifold. Namely, we require that regions in
\mathcal{X}
can be related to Banach spaces. However, unlike topological manifolds, we neither require that every such region is homeomorphic to the model Banach space, nor that these regions fit well together.

Figure 4
:
Feature decompositions do not need to have consistent topological dimension, nor do they need to be homeomorphic to any Banach space.

Instead, as illustrated in Figure
4
, we only require that
\mathcal{X}
can be decomposed into parts and that every such part can be embedded into a Banach space with the BAP. The Banach spaces linearize the parts of
\mathcal{X}
while the BAP gives us finite-dimensional approximations of points in possibly infinite-dimensional parts with arbitrarily small approximation error. We also require that the parts be disjoint, up to a negligible subset of
\mathcal{X}
, which we quantify by equipping
\mathcal{X}
with a measure.

Definition 3.4

(Feature Decomposition)
.

A feature decomposition of a metric measure space
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
, where
\mu
is a Borel probability measure on
\mathcal{X}
, is a countable collection of pairs
\{(\mathcal{X}_{n},\varphi_{n})\}_{n\leq N}

25
25

25

N=\infty
is allowed.
of non-empty closed subsets
\mathcal{X}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
and continuous injective maps
\varphi_{n}:\mathcal{X}_{n}\rightarrow F_{n}
into Banach “feature spaces”
F_{n}
with the BAP satisfying

(i)

Almost Disjoint:
each

\partial\mathcal{X}_{n}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\mathcal{X}_{n}\bigcap\underset{{m\neq n,\,m\leq N}}{\bigcup}\,\mathcal{X}_{m}

is a
\mu
-null set;

(ii)

Covering:
there exist
C_{\mu},r>0

such that

\mu\big(\bigcup_{m>n}\,\mathcal{X}_{m}\big)\leq C_{\mu}\,n^{-r}
for all
n
;
26
26

26

When
N<\infty
, we only require that
\mathcal{X}=\cup_{n\leq N}\mathcal{X}_{n}
.

(iii)

Ahlfors Regularity Near Boundaries:

For a subset
A\subseteq\mathcal{X}
, let
A_{R}
denote its
R
-neighborhood. There is a
C>0
such that for every
n\leq N
there is an
r_{n}>0
such that

\mu\big((\partial\mathcal{X}_{n})_{R}\cap\mathcal{X}_{n}\big)\leq C\mu(\mathcal{X}_{n})\,R^{r_{n}}

whenever
0\leq R\leq\operatorname{diam}(\mathcal{X}_{n})
.

(iv)

Non-Collapsing Diameter:

0<\inf_{n\leq N}\,\operatorname{diam}(\mathcal{X}_{n})
.

Each
\varphi_{n}
is called a feature map on part
\mathcal{X}_{n}
.

The following example illustrates that metric spaces can have a complicated and infinite-dimensional global geometry while admitting feature decompositions into simple finite-dimensional parts.

Example 3.5

(Infinitely Many Euclidean Spaces Glued at Origin)
.

Consider the set
\mathcal{X}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\sqcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}\,{\mathbb{R}}^{n}/\sim
with the identification
x\sim y
if and only if
x,y\in\{0_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
. Equip
\mathcal{X}
with the standard quotient metric

d_{\mathcal{X}}([x],[y])\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\begin{cases}\|x-y\|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}&:x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\\
\|x\|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}+\|y\|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{\tilde{n}}}&:x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\,y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\tilde{n}}\mbox{ and }n\neq\tilde{n}.\end{cases}

Let
\mu_{n}
denote the
n
-dimensional Lebesgue measure; we make
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})
into a metric measure space by equipping it with the measure

\mu(A)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\,\frac{1}{2^{3n/2}\pi^{n/2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}1_{A\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}(x)\,e^{-\|x\|^{2}/2}\mu_{n}(dx).

Since
\mu(\{[0]\})=0
we then conclude that
\{({\mathbb{R}}^{n}-\{0_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\},\text{Id}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
is a feature decomposition of
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
.
∎

Our next result applies to source spaces with combinatorial structure in the sense of Setting
3.6
(i), barycentric QAS target spaces, and
\omega
-Hölder-like target functions. In this case, via an application of the barycenter map, we simply obtain universal approximators
\hat{t}
from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{Y}
, as opposed to
\hat{T}
in Theorem
3.3
which maps from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})
.

When the feature decomposition of
\mathcal{X}
has more than one piece, the approximation guarantee is of a probably approximately correct (PAC)-type, meaning that it holds on a high-probability subset of
\mathcal{X}
with probability which depends quantitatively on
\mathcal{X}
’s geometry.

Although in this setting we proved a quantitative guarantee, here we give a qualitative statement for simplicity and because the rates coincide with our main quantitative result for general combinatorial
\mathcal{Y}
in Section
3.3
. A detailed quantitative version of the result for barycentric
\mathcal{Y}
in this section is given in Lemma
5.10
(see also Table
3
).

Setting 3.6

(Quantitative Setting: Combinatorial
\mathcal{X}
and Barycentric QAS
\mathcal{Y}
)
.

Assumptions:

(i)

(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
is a metric measure space with
\mu
supported on a compact subset
K\subseteq\mathcal{X}
, feature decomposition
\{(\mathcal{X}_{n},\varphi_{n})\}_{n\leq N}
, and either:

(a)

each
\varphi_{n}
is quasisymmetric and each
\operatorname{supp}(\mu)\cap\mathcal{X}_{n}
is doubling; and
\varphi_{n}|_{\varphi_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{n}\cap\operatorname{supp}(\mu))}^{-1}
is Hölder-like continuous for each
n\leq N
,

(b)

each
\varphi_{n}(\operatorname{supp}(\mu)\cap\mathcal{X}_{n})
is a doubling subset of
F_{n}
, and
\varphi_{n}|_{\varphi_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{n}\cap\operatorname{supp}(\mu))}^{-1}
is Hölder-like continuous for each
n\leq N
;

(ii)

for each
n\leq N
let
\{T_{k}^{(n)}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
realize the BAP on
\varphi_{n}(\operatorname{supp}(\mu)\cap\mathcal{X}_{n})
;

(iii)

(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}},\hat{\eta})
is a barycentric QAS space with quantized mixing function
\hat{\eta}
;

(iv)

\mathcal{F}_{\cdot}
is a universal approximator.

We can now state the result for barycentric
\mathcal{Y}
: any
\omega
-Hölder-like map from admissible
\mathcal{X}
to a barycentric QAS
\mathcal{Y}
can be approximated by piecing together Euclidean universal approximators.

Theorem 3.7

(Transfer Principle:
\mathcal{Y}
is a Barycentric QAS Space)
.

Assume Setting
3.6
. For any
\omega
-Hölder-like
f:K\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
and any
\varepsilon>0
, there exist an
N^{\star}\leq N
, positive integers

d_{n},c_{n},D_{n},N_{n}
for
n\leq N^{\star}
, vectors
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{N^{\star}}
with each
Z_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N_{n}\times D_{n}}
, functions
\hat{f}_{1},\dots,\hat{f}_{N^{\star}}
with
\hat{f}_{n}\in\mathcal{F}_{d_{n},N_{n},c_{n}}
,
a Borel subset
\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
with
\mu(\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon})\geq 1-\varepsilon
, and a Borel function
\hat{t}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
, such that

\hat{t}|_{\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}(x)=\beta_{\mathcal{Y}}\biggl(\sum_{n=1}^{N^{\star}}\,\psi_{n}(x)\,\delta_{\hat{\eta}\big(\hat{f}_{n}\circ\varphi^{(n)}(\cdot),Z_{n}\big)}\biggr),

where

\varphi^{(n)}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\iota_{T^{(n)}_{d_{n}}}^{-1}\circ T^{(n)}_{d_{n}}\circ\varphi_{n}:(\mathcal{X}_{n},d_{\mathcal{X}})\rightarrow({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{n}},\|\cdot\|_{F_{n}:d_{n}}),

(3.4)

\beta_{\mathcal{Y}}
denotes the barycenter map on
\mathcal{Y}
,
\psi_{n}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\dfrac{d_{\mathcal{X}}(x,\mathcal{X}_{n}^{c})}{\sum_{i\leq N^{\star}}\,d_{\mathcal{X}}(x,\mathcal{X}_{i}^{c})},

and
\hat{t}
satisfies

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}}\,d_{\mathcal{Y}}\big(\hat{t}(x),f(x)\big)<\varepsilon.

(3.5)

Moreover, if
N=1
then
\sup_{x\in K}\,d_{\mathcal{Y}}\big(\hat{t}(x),f(x)\big)<\varepsilon
where
K
denotes the support of
\mu
.

Proof.

The proof of a quantitative version of Theorem
3.7
, namely Lemma
5.10
, is given in Section
5
.
∎

Choosing a small value of
\varepsilon>0
in (
3.5
) simultaneously improves approximation quality and increases the size of
\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon}
, but requires a more complex model
\hat{f}
as quantified in Table
3
.

3.3
Quantitative Approximation: Combinatorial
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}

The Structure of Target Spaces

Let
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}},\hat{\eta})
be a QAS space with quantized mixing
27
27

27

See Section
2.2
for details on QAS spaces and Equation (
2.3
) for definition of a quantized mixing function.
function
\hat{\eta}
. Fix a subset
A\subseteq\mathcal{Y}
and a reference point
\bar{y}
in
A
, so that
(A,\bar{y})
is a
pointed subset
of
\mathcal{Y}
. As illustrated in Figure
5
, the mixing map
\eta
allows us to contract
A
towards
\bar{y}
,

A^{\delta}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\big\{\eta((1-\delta,\delta),(\bar{y},a)):\,a\in A\big\},

where
0<\delta\leq 1
is a parameter quantifying how much
A
is “pulled towards”
\bar{y}
. We call a pointed subset
(A,\bar{y})
of
\mathcal{Y}

\eta
-
geodesically convex
or simply
\eta
-
convex
if for all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
w\in\Delta_{k}
and
(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})\in A^{k}
one has
\eta(w,(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}))\in A
. In particular, if
A
is
\eta
-convex, then
A^{\delta}
is contained in
A
for every parameter
0\leq\delta\leq 1
.

Figure 5
:
Contracting points sets in
\mathcal{Y}
along “
\eta
-curves”.

We are interested in retractions of sets along geodesics because they allow us to subdivide
\mathcal{Y}
into pieces which are well-behaved and can be pulled away from one another by shrinking all geodesics in
\mathcal{Y}
.
The subdivisions that we consider are such that
\mathcal{Y}
is split into nearly disjoint pieces and the boundary of each piece is a probabilistic analogue of “topologically negligible boundaries” studied in the geometric topology literature
[
123
,
124
]
.

Definition 3.8

(Quantized Geodesic Partition)
.

Let
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}},\hat{\eta})
be a QAS metric space.
A quantized geodesic partition of
(\mathcal{Y},(Q_{q})_{q\in\mathbb{N}},\hat{\eta})
, whenever it exists, is a finite collection of closed and
\eta
-convex pointed subsets
\{(\mathcal{Y}_{m},\bar{y}_{m})\}_{m\leq M}
satisfying

(i)

for every
m,\tilde{m}\leq M
and
0\leq\delta<1
if
m\neq\tilde{m}
then
\mathcal{Y}_{m}^{\delta}\cap\mathcal{Y}_{\tilde{m}}=\emptyset
;

(ii)

\bigcup_{m\leq M}\,\mathcal{Y}_{m}=\mathcal{Y}
;

(iii)

each
(\mathcal{Y}_{m},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
is a barycentric metric space,

and admitting a monotonically decreasing continuous function
\boldsymbol{S}:[0,1]\rightarrow[0,\infty)
such that
\boldsymbol{S}(1)=0
,
\boldsymbol{S}(0)=\min_{m,\tilde{m}\leq M;\,m\neq\tilde{m}}\,d_{\mathcal{Y}}(\bar{y}_{m},\bar{y}_{\tilde{m}})
, and

\min_{m,\tilde{m}\leq M;\,m\neq\tilde{m}}\,d_{\mathbb{H}(\mathcal{Y})}(\mathcal{Y}_{m}^{\delta},\mathcal{Y}_{\tilde{m}}^{\delta})\geq\boldsymbol{S}(\delta),

for every
\delta\in[0,1]
. The pairs
(\mathcal{Y}_{m},\bar{y}_{m})
are called parts of
\mathcal{Y}
’s quantized geodesic partition.

The function
\boldsymbol{S}
in Definition (
3.8
) provides a lower-bound on the rate at which the retracted parts
\{\mathcal{Y}_{m}^{\delta}\}_{m\leq M}
are pulled apart as the parameter
\delta
varies. When the parameter
\delta
approaches either 0 or 1 the bound is tight.

Using geodesic partitions, we can quantify how likely a function is to cross the intersection “boundary-like region”
\mathcal{Y}_{m}\cap\mathcal{Y}_{\tilde{m}}
between any two parts
\mathcal{Y}_{m}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{\tilde{m}}
. The maps for which this can be quantified are called
geometrically stable maps
.

Definition 3.9

(Geometrically Stable Map)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
be a metric measure space, and
\{(\mathcal{Y}_{m},\bar{y}_{m})\}_{m\leq M}
a quantized geodesic partition of a QAS metric space
\mathcal{Y}
. A uniformly continuous
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
with Hölder-like modulus of continuity is said to be geometrically stable if there is a constant
C_{f}>0
such that for every
m\leq M
there is a constant
q_{m}>0
with

f_{\#}\mu\Big(\mathcal{Y}_{m}-\mathcal{Y}_{m}^{\delta}\Big)\leq C_{f}\,(1-\delta)^{q_{m}}\,f_{\#}\mu(\mathcal{Y}_{m})

for any
\delta\in[0,1)
.

Our main theorem in this subsection requires the following conditions.

Setting 3.10

(Quantitative Setting: Combinatorial
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
)
.

Assumptions:

(i)

(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}},\mu)
is a metric measure space with compactly supported
\mu
, feature decomposition
\{(\mathcal{X}_{n},\varphi_{n})\}_{n\leq N}
, and either:

(a)

each
\varphi_{n}
is quasisymmetric and each
\operatorname{supp}(\mu)\cap\mathcal{X}_{n}
is doubling; and
\varphi_{n}|_{\varphi_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{n}\cap\operatorname{supp}(\mu))}^{-1}
is Hölder-like continuous for each
n\leq N
,

(b)

each
\varphi_{n}(\operatorname{supp}(\mu)\cap\mathcal{X}_{n})
is a doubling subset of the feature space
28
28

28

See Definition
3.4
.

F_{n}
and
\varphi_{n}|_{\varphi_{n}(\mathcal{X}_{n}\cap\operatorname{supp}(\mu))}^{-1}
is Hölder-like continuous for each
n\leq N
;

(ii)

for each
n\leq N
,
\{T_{k}^{(n)}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
realizes the BAP on
\varphi_{n}(\operatorname{supp}(\mu)\cap\mathcal{X}_{n})
;

(iii)

the QAS space
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}},\hat{\eta})
admits a quantized geodesic partition
\{(\mathcal{Y}_{m},\bar{y}_{m})\}_{m\leq M}
;

(iv)

\mathcal{F}_{\cdot}
is a universal approximator.

We can now state our main quantitative result for
\omega
-Hölder-like functions between metric spaces satisfying the combinatorial conditions in Setting
3.10
.

Theorem 3.11

(Transfer Principle: Structure Case)
.

Assume Setting
3.10
. Let
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
be a
\omega
–Hölder–like continuous, geometrically stable map. For any quantization, encoding and approximation error
\varepsilon_{Q}
,
\varepsilon_{E}
and
\varepsilon_{A}>0
and any confidence level
0<\delta\leq 1
, there exist a positive number
0<{\varepsilon_{\star:A}}\leq\min\{\varepsilon_{A},\inf_{n\leq N}\,\operatorname{diam}(\mathcal{X}_{n}),1/2\}
, an
N^{\star}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, a Borel subset
\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon_{\star:A}}
, and a map
\hat{T}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})
satisfying

\mu\big(\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon_{\star:A}}\big)\geq 1-C_{0}\,\sum_{m\leq M}\,(1-\delta_{\star})^{q_{m}}\,f_{\#}\mu(\mathcal{Y}_{m})-C_{1}\,(N^{\star})^{-r}-\sum_{n\leq N^{\star}}\,C_{2}\,\mu\big(\mathcal{X}_{n}\big)\,\varepsilon_{\star:A}^{r_{n}}\geq 1-\delta,

(3.6)

such that the following estimate holds on
\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon_{\star:A}}

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon_{\star:A}}}W_{1}\big(\hat{T}(x),\delta_{f(x)}\big)<\varepsilon_{A}+\varepsilon_{Q}+\varepsilon_{E},

where
\delta_{\star}=\boldsymbol{S}^{\dagger}(3\varepsilon_{\star:A})
,
\boldsymbol{S}
is the function specified in Definition
3.8
,

r,r_{1},\dots,r_{N^{\star}}>0
are as in Definition
3.4
, and
q_{1},\dots,q_{M}>0
are as in Definition
3.9
. The quantities
\varepsilon_{\star:A}
,
N^{\star}
,
\delta_{\star}
depend only on
\varepsilon_{A}
and
\delta
, and
C_{0},C_{1},C_{2}>0
are independent of
\delta,\varepsilon_{A},\varepsilon_{E}
and
\varepsilon_{Q}
.
Moreover,
\hat{T}
admits the following representation on
\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon_{\star:A}}
:

\hat{T}(x)=\sum_{n\leq N^{\star}}\,\sum_{m\leq M}\,[C^{\mathcal{X}}(x)]_{n}\,[C^{\mathcal{Y}}(x)]_{m}\,\delta_{f^{(n,m)}(x)},

(3.7)

where the precise expressions for the maps
([C^{\mathcal{X}}]_{n})_{n\leq N^{\star}}
,
([C^{\mathcal{Y}}]_{m})_{m\leq M}
, and
f^{(n,m)}
are recorded in Table
2
. Estimates of the number of model parameters in terms of
\varepsilon_{A}
,
\varepsilon_{Q}
and
\varepsilon_{E}
are recorded in Table
3
.
Finally, let
\beta_{\mathcal{Y}_{m}}
be the contracting barycenter map on
(\mathcal{Y}_{m},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
. For any
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\varepsilon_{\star:A}}
, there exists an
m\leq M
such that
C^{\mathcal{Y}}(x)_{m}=1
,
\beta_{\mathcal{Y}_{m}}\circ\hat{T}(x)
is well-defined and it satisfies

d_{\mathcal{Y}}\big(\beta_{\mathcal{Y}_{m}}\circ\hat{T}(x),f(x)\big)<\varepsilon_{A}+\varepsilon_{Q}+\varepsilon_{E}.

Remark 3.12

), for

.

and
Z_{n}^{(m)}

\mathcal{Q}_{\cdot}^{m}
. In fact this is the case for any closed smooth submanifold in Euclidean space (which admits a triangulation), see Section

.

4
Applications

We consider four different classes of geometries that fit our theoretical framework:

1.

finite geometries, such as weighted graphs arising in computational geometry and theoretical computer science;

2.

non-smooth geometries arising in rough differential equations;

3.

infinite-dimensional linear geometries arising in inverse problems and partial differential equations;

4.

compact, smooth manifold geometries.

4.1
Finite Geometries

In this section, we apply our results to approximate functions between finite metric spaces induced by weighted graph structures, illustrated in Figure
6
. To this end, we briefly review some terminology.

Figure 6
:
Illustration of a randomized function between finite graphs.

Consider two weighted graphs
(V,E,W)
, where
V
are
vertices
,
E\subseteq V\times V
are
edges
, and
W:E\rightarrow(0,\infty)
are
edge weights
. We assume that both graphs are
connected
, meaning that for every pair of distinct vertices
v,u\in V
there is a sequence of edges
\big\{(v_{t},v_{t+1})\big\}_{t=1}^{T}\in E
with
v=v_{1}
and
v_{T}=u
; such sequences are called
paths
from
u
to
v
. In this case, both graphs can meaningfully be metrized with their shortest path metric, defined for any two nodes
u,v
in
V
by

d_{E}(u,v)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\min\,\sum_{t=1}^{T}\,W\big(\{v_{t},v_{t+1})\big),

where the minimum is computed over all paths from
u
to
v
.

4.1.1
Discretization of Riemannian Manifolds

Illustrated in Figure
7
, finite weighted graph approximations to compact and connected Riemannian manifolds are a classical tool in computational geometry when approximating the metric geometry of such manifolds. Currently, approximation rates are known
[
125
]
, and there are several streamlined algorithmic implementations
[
126
]
of graph approximation to manifolds equipped with a Riemannian distance function.

Figure 7
:
A function between discretizations of Riemannian manifolds, expressed as weighted graphs.

We take this as our starting point and consider two finite, connected weighted graphs
(\tilde{V},\tilde{E},\tilde{W})
and
(V,E,W)
together with a function
f:{V}\rightarrow\tilde{V}
between their vertices. If both weighted graphs can be discretizations of compact and connected Riemannian manifolds, where the vertices are points in these manifolds, the edges connect nearby points in the manifold, the weight given to each edge is the Riemannian distance between those pairs of points, and
f
can be taken to be a restriction of a smooth function between these spaces to the vertex sets. Since every function between finite metric spaces is Lipschitz, we deduce that
f
is Lipschitz.

Fix a positive integer
k
and let
\#\tilde{V}=k
.
The
1
-Wasserstein space
\mathcal{P}_{1}\big(\{1,\dots,k\}\big)
is bi-Lipschitz equivalent to the
\#\tilde{V}
-simplex with the
\ell_{1}
metric
30
30

30

This follows from the total-variation control of the
1
-Wasserstein (see
[
75
, Theorem 6.15]
) and the isometry between the total variation distance and the
\|\cdot\|_{1}
distance under the set map (
4.1
).

\big(\mathcal{P}\big(\tilde{V},d_{\tilde{E}}),W_{1}\big)\ni\sum_{u\in\tilde{V}}\,w_{u}\,\delta_{u}\mapsto(w_{u})_{u\in\tilde{V}}\in(\Delta_{\#\tilde{V}},\|\cdot\|_{1}).

(4.1)

Therefore, up to identification with the map (
4.1
),
f
’s “lift”
F\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\delta_{f(\cdot)}:(\tilde{V},d_{\tilde{E}})\rightarrow(\Delta_{\#\tilde{V}},\|\cdot\|_{1})
must also be Lipschitz with the same Lipschitz constant as
f
. There are various possible choices of injective feature maps, all of which will be bi-Lipschitz. However, a straightforward isometric feature map with finite-dimensional co-domain is given by the Fréchet–Kuratowski type embedding

\begin{aligned} \varphi:(V,d_{E})&\rightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{\#V},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\\
x&\mapsto\big(d_{E}(x,v)\big)_{v\in V}\end{aligned}.

(4.2)

In this case, Theorem
3.11
implies the following approximation result.

Corollary 4.1

(Universal Approximation of Maps Between Finite Graphs)
.

Let
(V,E,W)
and
(\tilde{V},\tilde{E},\tilde{W})
be finite, connected weighted graphs, and consider
f:V\rightarrow\tilde{V}
.
Fix a universal approximator
\mathcal{F}_{\cdot}
.
For every
\varepsilon>0
there is a
\hat{T}:(V,d_{E})\rightarrow\big(\mathcal{P}\big(\tilde{V},d_{\tilde{E}}),W_{1}\big)
with representation

\hat{T}(x)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\sum_{u\in\tilde{V}}\,[P_{\Delta_{\#\tilde{V}}}\circ\hat{f}\circ\big(d_{E}(x,v)\big)_{v\in V}]_{u}\,\delta_{u},

satisfying the uniform estimate

\max_{x\in V}\,W_{1}\big(\hat{T}(x),\delta_{f(x)}\big)<\varepsilon,

where
\hat{f}\in\bigcup_{c\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}\,\mathcal{F}_{\#V,\#\tilde{V},c}
.

In Corollary
4.1
there is not an obvious partition of
(V,E,W)
. The next example shows how such partitions arise for planar graphs. The approximation-theoretic advantage of partitioning is that small graphs embed into smaller Euclidean feature spaces with low distortion, leading to better approximation rates.

4.1.2
Approximating Colourings of Planar Graphs

A classical problem in graph theory, illustrated in Figure
8
, is that of
k
-colouring a finite planar graph
G=(V,E,W)
where
W(\{u,v\})=1
for every
\{u,v\}\in E
. We seek a function
f:V\rightarrow\{1,\dots,k\}
whose outputs we interpret as colours, such that no two adjacent vertices have the same colour.
If a colouring exists,
31
31

31

This is known for three and four colours
[
45
,
46
]
.
then our framework provides a means to approximate it.

Figure 8
:
Randomized approximations of graph colourings.

Note that
\{1,\dots,k\}
with the metric
|\cdot|
can be encoded as a weighted graph
(\tilde{V},\tilde{E},\tilde{W})
with vertices
\tilde{V}=\{i\}_{i=1}^{k}
, edges
\{(i,i+1)\}_{i=1}^{k-1}
, and constant weights
W(\{i,i+1\})=1
for every
i=1,\dots,k-1
. Therefore, the previous section’s considerations apply and, up to identification with the map (
4.1
), if a colouring
f:V\rightarrow\{1,\dots,K\}
exists, we may identify its “lift” with the map
F\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\delta_{f(\cdot)}:V\rightarrow\Delta_{k}
, to bi-Lipschitz equivalence of the target via (
4.1
).
We henceforth assume that a colouring
f
exists.

Feature decompositions of the source metric space organically arise in this problem, and their advantage is both approximation-theoretic and computational. To see the later advantage, observe that in practice, most graphs
G
contain a large number of vertices and edges connecting those vertices; thus, it can be computationally challenging even to compute
d_{G}
. However, the under within structure on
G
implies (
[
127
, Theorem 4]
) that there exist more than disjoint two subsets of vertices
V_{1},\dots,V_{I}
such that
\bigcup_{i=1}^{I}\,V_{i}=V
and with the striking property if we define sub-graphs
G_{i}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}(V_{i},E_{i})
where
E_{i}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\{\{v,u\}\in E:\,u,v\in V_{i}\}
then

d_{G}(v,u)=d_{G_{i}}(v,u),

(4.3)

for every
u,v\in V_{i}
for each
i=1,\dots,I
. Moreover, such partitions can be computed in quadratic time. Now since each
G_{i}
can contain far fewer points then the original graph
G
and since the inclusion
(V_{i},d_{G_{i}})
into
(V,d_{G})
is isometric, there is no change to the original problem from a metric theoretic perspective.

When we build our feature maps, the approximation-theoretic advantage emerges from simple dimensional considerations. For every
i\leq I
, we consider the feature spaces
F_{\varphi_{i}}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\big(\mathbb{R}^{\#V_{i}},\|\cdot\|_{\infty}\big)
where the feature maps
\varphi_{i}:V_{i}\rightarrow F_{\varphi_{i}}
are given by the analogous embeddings to (
4.2
) but only performed locally on each sub-graph
G_{1},\dots,G_{I}
; that is,

\varphi_{i}(u)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\big(d_{G_{i}}(u,v)\big)_{v\in V_{i}}.

(4.4)

We note that
\varphi_{i}(u)=\big(d_{G}(u,v)\big)_{v\in V_{i}}
.

The approximation-theoretic advantage of partitioning can naturally be explained in the context of deep learning with inputs on planar graphs.
Suppose that
\mathcal{F}_{\cdot}
is the set of deep feedforward neural networks with ReLU activation function like in Example
2.12
. Sufficiently wide neural networks in
\mathcal{F}_{\cdot}
of depth
\mathcal{O}(c)
approximate arbitrary Lipschitz functions from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}
at a rate of
\mathcal{O}(c^{2/d})
, uniformly on compact sets
[
59
]
. Since each sub-graph
G_{i}
of
G
contains strictly fewer vertices, approximating
f|_{G_{i}}\circ\varphi_{i}^{-1}
with
I
sufficiently wide neural networks of depth
\mathcal{O}(c)
achieves an approximation error of
\mathcal{O}(c^{2/{\#V_{i}}})
, using a total of
\mathcal{O}(Ic)
parameters across all the
I
neural networks. In contrast, without partitioning, approximating
f\circ\varphi
by a sufficiently wide neural network of depth
\mathcal{O}(c)
only achieves an error of
\mathcal{O}(c^{2/d})
. Thus, by partitioning, a
linear
increase in the number of parameters used to approximate
f
on each of the sub-graphs
G_{i}
results in an exponential reduction in uniform approximation error.

Corollary 4.2

(Universal Approximation of Stochastically Continuous
k
-Colouring’s)
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a finite planar graph, let
(G_{i})_{i=1}^{I}
,

G_{i}=(V_{i},E_{i})
be sub-graphs of
G
satisfying (
4.3
),
k
be a positive integer, and suppose that there exists a
k
-colouring
f:V\rightarrow\{1,\dots,k\}
of
G
. For every
\varepsilon>0
there is a
\hat{T}:V\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{1}(\{1,\dots,k\})
with representation

\hat{T}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\,\psi_{i}(x)\,\Big(\sum_{v\in V_{i}}\,[P_{\Delta_{\#V_{i}}}\,f_{i}\circ d_{G_{i}}(x,v)]_{v}\,\delta_{v}\Big)\qquad\mbox{ and }\qquad\psi_{i}(x)=\frac{d_{G}(x,V_{i}^{c})}{\sum_{j=1}^{I}\,d_{G}(x,V_{j}^{c})}

satisfying the uniform estimate

\max_{x\in V}\,W_{1}\big(\hat{T}(x),\delta_{f(x)}\big)<\varepsilon.

4.1.3
Classification

We now consider the binary classification problem in classical (Euclidean) machine learning, illustrated in Figure
9
. Suppose we are given a pair of random variables
X
and
Y
, with
Y
taking values in the discrete metric space
\{0,1\}
, with distance
d_{\mathcal{Y}}(0,1)=1
, and
X
taking values in the
d
-dimensional Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for some positive integer
d
.
By the disintegration theorem (see
[
128
, Theorem 6.3]
), we know that there exists a regular conditional distribution function (that is, a Markov kernel)
\mathbb{P}(Y|X=\cdot)
describing the conditional law of
Y
given
X
, that is a measurable function from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
into the space of probability measures on
\mathcal{Y}
.
The Bayes classifier
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
is defined as a measurable selection
32
32

32

E.g. Suppose that
d=1
,
(W_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a Brownian motion,
X=W_{1}
, and that
Y=I_{[0,\infty)}(W_{2}-X)
. I.e.
Y
classifies that the Brownian motion goes up or down at the next increment. Since
(W_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a martingale, then both states
0
or
1
can happen with equal probabilities; thus, there is not a unique Bayes classifier for this problem.

\mathbb{P}(Y|X=x)(\{f(x)\})=\max_{i=0,1}\,\mathbb{P}(Y|X=x)(\{i\}).

(4.5)

Since all continuous functions from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\{0,1\}
are constants, no Bayes classifier can be uniformly approximated on
all
compact sets by any set of continuous functions. However, the Markov kernel
\mathbb{P}(Y|X=\cdot)
is always a measurable function from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to the space of probability measures
\mathcal{P}(\{0,1\})
and it is often continuous when
\mathcal{P}(\{0,1\})
is metrized by the Wasserstein metric
33
33

33

In information theory, one often encounters the total variation. We note that they are bi-Lipschitz equivalent in this case.
.
Thus most formulations of the classification problem instead consider the relaxed problem, illustrated by Figure
9
, of approximating the regular conditional distribution/Markov kernel
\mathbb{P}(Y|X=\cdot)
; under the implicit assumption that
x\mapsto\mathbb{P}(Y|X=x)
is continuous or even Lipschitz
34
34

34

E.g. Suppose that
d=1
,
(W_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a Brownian motion,
X=W_{1}
, and that
Y=I_{[0,\infty)}(W_{2})
. Then,
\mathbb{P}(Y|X=\cdot)
is Lipschitz. A similar argument holds for most strong solutions to stochastic differential equations with uniform Lipschitz dynamics, and this follows from classical stability estimates (see
[
129
, Propositions 8.15 and 8.16]
).
.

Figure 9
:
Classification is typically performed by lifting the target space
\{0,1\}
to probability measures on
\{0,1\}
and then approximating the lifted map.

We note that the source space,
\mathcal{X}=(\mathbb{R}^{d},\|\cdot\|_{\ell^{2}_{d}})
, admits the trivial feature decomposition
\{(\mathbb{R}^{d},1_{\mathbb{R}^{d}})\}
; wherein
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is its own feature space. The target space is
(\mathcal{P}_{1}(\{0,1\},\mathcal{W}_{1})
on
\{0,1\}
, whose elements are in correspondence with the Euclidean
2
-simplex via the bi-Lipschitz
35
35

35

This follows from the fact that
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\{0,1\}
metrized with the total variation metric is isometric to the Euclidean
2
-simplex and from standard estimates between the total variation Wasserstein distances in such contexts (see
[
75
]
).
identification
w_{1}\delta_{0}+w_{2}\delta_{1}\leftrightarrow(w_{1},w_{2})
. This is a QAS space with mixing function
\eta(w,\mathbb{P}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{P}_{N})\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\,w_{n}\mathbb{P}_{n}
and quantized by

\mathcal{Q}_{1}(z)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\mathcal{Q}_{1}(z)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\sigma(z)\,\delta_{0}+(1-\sigma(z))\,\delta_{1},

where
\sigma
is a re-scaled “hard sigmoid” function of
[
130
]
given by
\sigma(u)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\min\{\max\{u,1\},0\}
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{Y}
admits the “trivial” quantized geodesic partition
\{([0,1],0)\}
.
Thus, if
\mathcal{F}_{\cdot}
is the universal approximator of Example
2.12
, then for Lipschitz Markov kernels, Theorem
3.11
implies the following “universal classification theorem”.

Corollary 4.3

(Universal Classification)
.

In the notation of this subsection, assume that the Markov kernel
\mathbb{P}(Y|X=\cdot)
is Lipschitz. For every
\varepsilon,r>0
there exist a map
\hat{f}
(a ReLU network), as in Example
2.12
, satisfying

\max_{\|x\|\leq r}\,W_{1}\Big(\mathbb{P}(Y|X=x),\sigma(\hat{f}(x))\,\delta_{0}+(1-\sigma(\hat{f}(x))\,\delta_{1}\Big)<\varepsilon.

Remark 4.4
.

In the binary classification literature, one typically only considers the weight of
\delta_{1}
produced by the typical “deep classifier” model in Corollary (
4.3
), since the weight assigned to
\delta_{1}
can be automatically inferred.

Remark 4.5

(Lower Regularity Kernels)
.

The Markov kernel being approximated in Corollary
4.3
is always measurable. Therefore, if one cannot assume that it is Lipschitz, then for any “prior probability measure”
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for example the standard Gaussian probability measure, one can infer from Lusin’s theorem that for
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
there is a compact subset
K
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
on which the Markov kernel is continuous and for which
\mu(K)>1-\varepsilon
. Therefore, Theorem
3.3
applies, from which we deduce
36
36

36
