This type of guarantee is called a PAC (probably approximately correct) approximability guarantee in machine learning.
that

\mu\big(W_{1}\Big(\mathbb{P}(Y|X=x),\sigma(\hat{f}(x))\,\delta_{0}+(1-\sigma(\hat{f}(x))\,\delta_{1}\Big)>\varepsilon\big)\geq 1-\varepsilon.

Next, we consider the implications of our theory for non-finite metric spaces when at least one of the involved geometries is a finite-dimensional (in the sense of Assouad) non-smooth metric space.

4.2
Non-Smooth Geometries

We consider two classes of examples. First, we consider a broad class of finite-dimensional non-smooth metric geometries on the target space, which can arise in the context of
\operatorname{CAT}(0)
spaces, injective metric spaces, and ultralimits thereof.
Second, we consider a non-smooth metric geometry on the source space, which arises from rough path theory. In this case, we approximate the solution operator to rough differential equations.

4.2.1
Quantizable Metric Spaces with Conical Geodesic Bicombings are QAS Spaces

In a (separable and infinite-dimensional) Hilbert space or on a Cartan–Hadamard manifold, any two points can be joined by a unique distance-minimizing geodesic. This is not the case in general geodesic metric spaces, for which, at best, one must choose which of the multiple distance-minimizing geodesics to use when connecting a pair of points.

Figure 10
:
Visualization of the mixing function
\hat{\eta}
in (
4.7
) when
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
is an
\mathbb{R}
-trees. The map
\eta
considers the unique point on
\mathcal{Y}
which is closest to the given points
y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}
in
\mathcal{Y}
, where the relative importance of each point is quantified by the “weight”
w\in\Delta_{3}
. This is done by first going up to the
1
-Wasserstein space over
\mathcal{Y}
and mixing the lifted points
\delta_{y_{1}},\delta_{y_{2}},\delta_{y_{3}}
according to weight
w
via the mixing function (
2.5
); resulting in the measure
w_{1}\delta_{y_{1}}+w_{2}\delta_{y_{2}}+w_{3}\delta_{y_{3}}
. Then, we go back down to
\mathcal{Y}
by identifying that measure’s barycenter.

Naturally, this leads to working with a distinguished selection of geodesics of a metric space
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
, called a
geodesic bicombing

[
131
]
. A geodesic bicombing is a map
\sigma:\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Y}\times[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
with the property that for every
x,\tilde{x}\in\mathcal{Y}
we have
\sigma(x,\tilde{x},0)=x
,
\sigma(x,\tilde{x},1)=\tilde{x}
and
d_{\mathcal{Y}}\big(\sigma(x,\tilde{x},t),\sigma(x,\tilde{x},\tilde{t})\big)=|t-\tilde{t}|d_{\mathcal{Y}}(x,\tilde{x})
.
The barycentricity condition imposes the main geometric constraint on the space
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
. One is typically interested in complete metric spaces admitting a
conical
geodesic bicombing by which we mean a geodesic bicombing satisfying the convexity-like property
37
37

37

The convexity of the map
t\mapsto d_{\mathcal{Y}}(\eta(x,\tilde{x},t),\eta(x^{\prime},\tilde{x}^{\prime},t))
, for all
x,\tilde{x},x^{\prime},\tilde{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}
implies but is not equivalent to
\eta
being conical (see
[
132
, Proposition 3.8]
).

d_{\mathcal{Y}}(\sigma(x,\tilde{x},t),\sigma(x^{\prime},\tilde{x}^{\prime},t))\leq(1-t)d_{\mathcal{Y}}(x,x^{\prime})+td_{\mathcal{Y}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{x}^{\prime}).

(4.6)

This is because
[
133
, Theorem 2.6]
characterizes metric spaces admitting a
1
-Lipschitz barycenter map
\beta:\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
as precisely being those which admit a conical geodesic bicombing. Examples of metric spaces admitting conical geodesic bicombings, are injective metric spaces, real trees, Cartan-Hadamard manifolds, Banach spaces,
\operatorname{CAT}(0)
spaces, and several other spaces (see
[
134
,
135
,
136
]
).

Access to such a barycenter map allows us to define a mixing function
\eta:\hat{\mathcal{Y}}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
in two phases. For any given
(w,(y_{i})_{i=1}^{N})\in\hat{\mathcal{Y}}
, we first lift the mixing problem to the
1
-Wasserstein space
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})
where it can be solved by assembling the finitely supported measure
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\,w_{i}\,\delta_{y_{i}}
. Then, the barycenter map
\beta
uniquely identifies a point on
\mathcal{Y}
which is closest to each
y_{1},\dots,y_{N}
, where the relative importance of each point is quantified by the measure
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\,w_{i}\delta_{y_{i}}
.
As illustrated by Figure
10
, for each
(w,(y_{i})_{i=1}^{N})\in\hat{\mathcal{Y}}
we define

\eta\big(w,(y_{i})_{i=1}^{N}\big)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\beta\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\,w_{i}\,\delta_{y_{i}}\right).

(4.7)

The
1
-Lipschitzness of the barycenter map
\beta
implies that (
2.2
) holds with
C_{\eta}=1
and
p=1
.

If, moreover,
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
is quantizable via some quantization
\mathcal{Q}_{\cdot}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\{\mathcal{Q}_{q}:\mathbb{R}^{D_{q}}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}\}
, then (
4.7
) can be used to construct a quantized mixing function
\hat{\eta}:\bigcup_{N,q\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
, defined by,

\hat{\eta}(w,(z_{i})_{i=1}^{N})\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\beta\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\,w_{i}\,\delta_{Q_{q}(z_{i})}\right).

(4.8)

From these considerations, we deduce that any complete quantizable metric space admitting a conical geodesic bicombing is a viable target space in the context of Theorem
3.7
.

Proposition 4.6

(Quantizable Metric Spaces with Conical Geodesic Bicombings are QAS Spaces)
.

Let
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
be a complete metric space with a conical geodesic bicombing
\eta
and admitting a quantization
\mathcal{Q}
. Then the triple
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
is a QAS space, with quantized mixing function
\hat{\eta}
defined in (
4.8
).

A simple non-trivial example arises from the finite-dimensional space
(\mathbb{R}^{d},\|\cdot\|_{1})
used in sparse learning.

Example 4.7

(
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\ell^{1}
norm)
.

Let
d
be a positive integer. The finite-dimensional Banach space
(\mathbb{R}^{d},\|\cdot\|_{1})
does not admit unique geodesics between any two distinct points, but
\sigma(w,y_{1},y_{2})\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}w_{1}y_{1}+w_{2}y_{2}
is a conical geodesic bicombing, where
w\in[0,1]
and
y_{1},y_{2}
are vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If we consider the “trivial” quantization
\mathcal{Q}_{\cdot}
where for each
q
we set
D_{q}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}d
and
\mathcal{Q}_{q}(z)=z
, then the quantized mixing function in (
4.8
) is nothing more than the weighted average of any finite set of vectors
z_{1},\dots,z_{N}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with weight
w\in\Delta_{N}

\hat{\eta}(w,(z_{i})_{i=1}^{N})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\,w_{i}\,z_{i}.

This is because the
1
-Lipschitz barycenter map
\beta
on a Banach space is the Bochner integral (see
[
80
]
).
∎

We now consider a non-linear example relevant in stochastic analysis
[
137
]
and computer vision
[
53
]
.

Example 4.8

(Symmetric Positive Definite Matrices)
.

Let
d
be a positive integer. Let
\operatorname{SPD}_{d}
be the smooth manifold of
d\times d
symmetric positive definite matrices equipped with Riemannian metric
g(X,Y)_{\Sigma}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\operatorname{tr}\big(\Sigma^{-1}X\Sigma^{-1}Y\big)
where
\Sigma\in\operatorname{SPD}_{d}
and where
X,Y
belong to
\Sigma
’s tangent space which is identified with the set of
d\times d
symmetric matrices
\operatorname{Sym}_{d}
. This is a Cartan–Hadamard manifold, with a unique geodesic bicombing given by the weighted geometric mean
\sigma(w,\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2})\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}(\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2})^{w_{1}}\Sigma_{1}^{1/2}
, where
\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}\in\operatorname{SPD}_{d}
and
w\in\Delta_{2}
(see
[
138
]
). By
[
139
, Propositions 3.1 and 6.1]
,
(\operatorname{SPD}_{d},d_{g})
is barycentric for its geodesic distance

d_{g}(\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2})=\big\|\log(\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1/2})\big\|_{F},

where
\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}\in\operatorname{SPD}_{d}
,
\log
is the matrix logarithm, and
\|\cdot\|_{F}
is the Fröbenius norm. Exploiting the fixed-point characterization of its barycenter map in
[
140
, Theorem 3.1]
38
38

38

The barycenters in
(\operatorname{SPD}_{d},g)
are solutions to the Karcher equation
\int_{\Sigma\in\operatorname{SPD}_{d}}\,\log(\Sigma^{1/2}\tilde{\Sigma}\Sigma^{1/2})\,\mathbb{P}(d\tilde{\Sigma})=0
where
\mathbb{P}
is the finitely supported probability measure
\mathbb{P}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\,w_{i}\,\Sigma_{i}
(see
[
141
,
142
,
140
]
).
implies that a mixing function
\eta
on
\operatorname{SPD}_{d}
is

\eta\big(w,(\Sigma_{i})_{i=1}^{N}\big)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\min_{\Sigma\in\operatorname{SPD}_{d}}\,\biggl\|\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}\,\log\big(\Sigma^{-1/2}\Sigma_{i}\Sigma^{-1/2}\big)\biggr\|_{F},

where
\Sigma_{1}\dots,\Sigma_{N}\in\operatorname{SPD}_{d}
and
w\in\Delta_{N}
. We obtain a quantized mixing function by the Cartan-Hadamard Theorem since the Riemannian exponential map at the identity matrix is a diffeomorphism from
\operatorname{Sym}_{d}
to
\operatorname{SPD}_{D}
which is equal to the matrix exponential
\exp
. Upon identifying
39
39

39

The identification sends any vector
z\in\operatorname{R}^{d(d+1)/2}
to the symmetric matrix whose upper-triangular part is populated by
z
’s entries.

\operatorname{Sym}_{d}
with
\mathbb{R}^{d(d+1)/2}
, we deduce that

\hat{\eta}(w,(z_{i})_{i=1}^{N})\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\min_{\Sigma\in\operatorname{SPD}_{d}}\,\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}\,\log\big(\Sigma^{-1/2}\exp(z_{i})\Sigma^{-1/2}\big)\Big\|_{F}

is a quantized mixing function on
(\operatorname{SPD}_{d},d_{g})
; where
z_{1},\dots,z_{N}\in\operatorname{R}^{d(d+1)/2}
and
w\in\Delta_{N}
.
∎

We now apply this to construct universal approximators of solutions to rough differential equations.

4.2.2
Carnot Groups and Rough Differential Equations

Before showing how our results can be used to approximate solution operators to rough differential equations, we begin by summarizing the driving concepts behind the theory of rough paths.

Rough Path Theory

The core idea of rough path theory
[
54
]
is to make precise the meaning of the (rough) differential equation

dy_{t}=V(y_{t})dx_{t},\quad y(0)=y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{e},

(4.9)

when the driving signal
x
is not smooth, for example, when
x\in C^{\alpha}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
0<\alpha<1/2
. Formally, the Euler scheme shows that given any sufficiently smooth vector field
V=(V_{i})_{1\leq i\leq d}\in\text{Lip}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{e},L(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{e}))
40
40

40

V\in\text{Lip}^{\gamma}
means that
V
is
[\gamma]
–times continuously differentiable and its
[\gamma]^{th}
derivative is
(\gamma-[\gamma])
–Hölder continuous.
with
\gamma>1/\alpha
, an integer
41
41

41

Here
\lfloor a\rfloor
stands for the integer part of a real
a
.

N\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\big\lfloor\frac{1}{\alpha}\big\rfloor\geq 2
, and any time
s<t\in[0,T]
, it holds that

\Bigg|y(t)-y(s)-\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1,\ldots,d}V_{i_{1}}\ldots V_{i_{k}}I(y(s))\int_{s<r_{1}<\ldots<r_{k}<t}dx^{i_{1}}_{r_{1}}\ldots dx^{i_{k}}_{r_{k}}\Bigg|\lesssim(t-s)^{\frac{\gamma}{\alpha}};

(4.10)

here, the iterated integrals
\int_{s<r_{1}<\ldots<r_{k}<t}dx^{i_{1}}_{r_{1}}\ldots dx^{i_{k}}_{r_{k}}
are well–defined for the rough signal,
x
. Since
\gamma>1/\alpha
, the remainder term of the left–hand side of (
4.10
) is of order
o(t-s)
and, consequently, one can approximate the solution
y(t)
to (
4.9
) by a Riemannian sum,
y(t)=y(0)+\lim_{|\mathcal{P}|\to 0}\sum_{s<t\in\mathcal{P}}\mathcal{E}(y(s),\mathbf{X}_{s,t})
, where
|\mathcal{P}|
is the mesh size of a partition
\mathcal{P}
of the time-window
[0,T]
, where

\mathbf{X}_{s,t}=1+\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1,\ldots,d}\int_{s<r_{1}<\ldots<r_{k}<t}dx^{i_{1}}_{r_{1}}\ldots dx^{i_{k}}_{r_{k}}e_{i_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes e_{i_{k}}

(4.11)

belongs to the truncated free (tensor) algebra
\in T^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes k}
and where

\mathcal{E}(y(s),\mathbf{X}_{s,t})=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}=1,\ldots,d}V_{i_{1}}\ldots V_{i_{k}}I(y(s))\int_{s<r_{1}<\ldots<r_{k}<t}dx^{i_{1}}_{r_{1}}\ldots dx^{i_{k}}_{r_{k}}.

From this observation,
[
54
]
introduced the notion of a rough differential equation (RDE). Briefly, the author asserts that solving an ODE (
4.9
) driven by rough signal
x\in C^{\alpha}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})
42
42

42

For “roughness” we usually mean
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
so that the Young’s integral cannot be defined.
on Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{e}
is equivalent to solving a so-called (full) RDE

d\mathbf{Y}_{t}=V(y_{t})\,d\mathbf{X}_{t}

(4.12)

driven by a (geometric) rough path
\mathbf{X}\in C^{\alpha}([0,T],G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
on a certain manifold
G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})\subseteq T^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})
. The algebraic properties which the iterated integral in (
4.11
) must satisfy imply that
G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a certain Lie group, namely, it must be the free nilpotent Carnot group over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
of step
N
. Thus, the “rough signal”
\mathbf{X}
in (
4.12
), which mimics the iterated integrals (
4.11
), takes values in this specific manifold.
We point the reader to
[
143
, Chapter 7]
for a precise description of these Carnot groups. Here we mainly need the fact that
G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
possesses a natural sub–Riemannian structure which induces the Carnot–Carathéodory metric
d_{CC}
.

Consider a “low-regularity”
\alpha
–Hölder continuous path in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with
0<\alpha<1/2
. Then, the iterated integrals
\int_{s<r_{1}<\ldots<r_{k}<t}dx^{i_{1}}_{r_{1}}\ldots dx^{i_{k}}_{r_{k}}
cannot exist in any classical sense. One of the main contributions of the rough path theory is to provide a method for lifting every such rough curve
x\in C^{\alpha}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})
to a so–called (geometric) rough path
\mathbf{X}
. In
[
144
]
, it is shown that these geometric rough paths take values in
G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, just like the classical iterated integrals do for smooth curves while also preserving the regularity of
x
.

Theorem 4.9

(
[
144
, Theorem 14]
)
.

For every regularity
0<\alpha<1/2
and each path
x\in C^{\alpha}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})
, there is an
\mathbf{X}\in C^{\alpha}([0,T],(G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),d_{CC}))
such that
\pi_{1}(\mathbf{X})=x
, where
\pi_{1}:T^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\oplus_{k=0}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes k}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the canonical projection onto the first component of any tensor therein.

Any
\mathbf{X}
guaranteed by Theorem
4.9
is referred to as a (geometric) rough path over
x
. The solution to RDE (
4.9
) and (
4.12
) can be meaningfully constructed under the following assumptions.

Definition 4.10
.

Assume that the following hold

(i)

x\in C^{\alpha}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
,
\frac{1}{\alpha}\notin\mathbb{N}
, be the driven signal in (
4.9
);

(ii)

V=(V_{i})_{1\leq i\leq d}
be a vector field such that
V_{i}\in\text{Lip}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{e},\mathbb{R}^{e})
for
i=1,\ldots,d
for some
\gamma>0
;

(iii)

y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{e}
,
\mathbf{y}_{0}\in G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})
with
\pi_{1}(\mathbf{y}_{0})=y_{0}
;

(iv)

\mathbf{X}\in C^{\alpha}([0,T],(G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),d_{CC}))
is a (geometric) rough path above
x
.

Then

(a)

A (geometric) rough path
\mathbf{Y}\in C^{\alpha}([0,T],(G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e}),d_{CC}))
is called a full RDE solution to (
4.12
) with initial value
\mathbf{y}_{0}
, if there exists a sequence
(x^{n})\subseteq\text{Lip}^{1}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\sup_{n}\|S_{N}(x^{n})\|_{\alpha}<\infty
43
43

43

Here
\|\cdot\|_{\alpha}
denotes the
\alpha
–Hölder norm of
G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
–valued curve with respect to
d_{CC}
.
,
\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{s<t\in[0,T]}d_{CC}(S_{N}(x^{n})_{s,t},\mathbf{X}_{s,t})=0
, so that the sequence of solutions
y^{n}
to the ODEs
dy^{n}_{t}=V(y^{n}_{t})dx^{n}_{t}
,
y^{n}_{0}=y_{0}
satisfy that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{n}d_{CC}(\mathbf{y}_{0}\otimes S_{N}(y^{n})_{t},\mathbf{Y}_{t})=0
.

(b)

Let
\mathbf{Y}
be a full RDE solution with initial value
\mathbf{y}_{0}
in the above sense. Then
y=\pi_{1}(\mathbf{Y})
is called a RDE solution to (
4.9
) with initial value
y_{0}
.

The following theorem regarding the regularity of the flow map associated with full RDEs, also called the
Itô–Lyons map
in stochastic analysis, is well known in the rough path theory, see, e.g.
[
143
, Chapter 10]
.

Theorem 4.11

(
[
143
, Theorem 10.41]
)
.

With the notations and assumptions from Definition
4.10
and assuming that
V\in\text{Lip}^{\gamma}
with
\gamma>\frac{1}{\alpha}
, there exists a unique full RDE solution to (
4.12
) (and therefore a unique RDE solution to (
4.9
)). Moreover, let
|\cdot|_{T^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})}
denote the normal Euclidean norm on
T^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})
, the flow
\mathcal{I}^{V,\mathbf{X}}_{0\to T}:(G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e}),d_{CC})\to(G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e}),|\cdot|_{T^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})})
,
\mathbf{y}\mapsto\mathbf{Y}^{\mathbf{y}}_{T}
for
\mathbf{Y}^{\mathbf{y}}_{T}
being the full RDE solution to (
4.12
) with initial value
\mathbf{y}
, is Lipschitz continuous: there exists a constant
C=C(N,d,\|\mathbf{X}\|_{\alpha},|V|_{\text{Lip}^{\gamma}})
such that for all
\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}\in G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})
it holds that

\Big|\mathcal{I}^{V,\mathbf{X}}_{0\to T}(\mathbf{y})-\mathcal{I}^{V,\mathbf{X}}_{0\to T}(\mathbf{z})\Big|_{T^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})}\leq Cd_{CC}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}).

The map
\mathcal{I}^{V,\mathbf{X}}_{0\to T}
is referred to as the
flow of the rough differential equation
(
4.12
).

Remark 4.12
.

The solution to a RDE or a full RDE depends crucially on the choice of the rough path lift
\mathbf{X}
of the underlying
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
–valued path
x
; in general, this choice is not unique. However, if
x
is a realization of a stochastic process
X
with sufficiently regular trajectories (e.g. if
x
is a continuous semimartingale) then, as a classical choice,
\mathbf{X}
can simply be taken to be the iterated Stratonovich integrals. In such cases, RDE’s solution coincides with the classical strong solution to stochastic differential equation (SDE) a.s.. The advantage of the rough path theoretic approach is that it provides a pathwise stability estimate on an SDE’s solution which are unavailable by classical probabilistic tools. For a comprehensive treatment of rough path theory, we refer readers to
[
145
]
,
[
143
]
, or
[
146
]
and for applications of rough path theory in stochastic analysis, time series, mathematical finance, and machine learning we refer to the 2014 ICM expository monograph
[
147
]
.

Theorem
3.3
is the first result showing that the flow of an RDE,
\mathcal{I}^{V,\mathbf{X}}_{0\to T}
.
\mathcal{I}^{V,\mathbf{X}}_{0\to T}
, can be uniformly approximated on compact sets, quantitatively.

Source space

The set
\mathcal{X}=G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})
,
d_{CC}
is the Carnot–Carathéodory metric, and
\mu
is a Borel probability measure on
\mathcal{X}
with compact support. Since the Carnot group
(G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e}),d_{CC})
is a doubling metric space
[
148
]
, if
K
denotes the doubling constant, Naor and Neimann’s quantitative formulation of Assouad’s embedding theorem (
[
149
, Theorem 1.2]
) implies that, for every
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
there exist an
n=n(K)
, a
D=D(K,\varepsilon)
and an embedding
\varphi_{n,\varepsilon}:G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})\to R^{n}
such that for all
\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}\in G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})
,

d_{CC}^{1-\varepsilon}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z})\leq|\varphi_{n,\varepsilon}(\mathbf{y})-\varphi_{n,\varepsilon}(\mathbf{z})|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\leq Dd_{CC}^{1-\varepsilon}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}).

Fix some
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
and set
\beta:=1-\varepsilon
, then the above shows that
G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})
with the snowflake metric
d_{\mathcal{X}}=d_{CC}^{\beta}
admits a bi-Lipschitz feature map, namely
\varphi_{n,\varepsilon}
, with feature space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and the usual Euclidean norm
|\cdot|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
. Note that
\mathbb{R}^{n}
clearly has the BAP and, for any compact subset thereof, we can take the finite-rank operators
(T_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
to be the identity maps thereon.
Lastly, fix any non-empty compact subset
\mathcal{K}
of
G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})
and let
\mu
be any Borel probability measure thereon.

Target space

The set
\mathcal{Y}=G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})
. Then we know that
\log:G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})\to\mathfrak{g}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})
is a diffeomorphism onto a finite-dimensional Euclidean space (see
[
143
, Theorem 7.30]
), therefore is locally bi–Lipschitz when both
G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})\subseteq T^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})
and
\mathfrak{g}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})\subseteq T^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})
are equipped with the Euclidean norm
|\cdot|_{T^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})}
. Now we define
d_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z})\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}|\log(\mathbf{y})-\log(\mathbf{z})|_{T^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})}
. Clearly,
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
is a QAS space with one single quantization map
Q=\exp:\mathbb{R}^{q}\cong\mathfrak{g}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})
for
q=\text{dim}(\mathfrak{g}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e}))
and the conical geodesic bicombing computed its “Euclidean tangent space” as

\eta(w,\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z})\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\exp(w_{1}\log(\mathbf{y})+w_{2}\log(\mathbf{z})).

The mixing function
\eta
and its quantized version can be chosen as in (
4.7
).

Regularity of the target function

The target function to be approximated is the flow
\mathcal{I}^{V,\mathbf{X}}_{0\to T}:(G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e}),d_{CC})\to(G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e}),|\cdot|_{T^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d}})
of the RDE (
4.12
). This map fulfills the regularity requirements of the quantitative approximation result in Theorem
3.7
: since

\mathbf{y}\mapsto\mathbf{Y}^{\mathbf{y}}_{T}
is Lipschitz continuous by Theorem
4.11
,
d_{\mathcal{X}}=d_{CC}^{\beta}
, and
\text{Id}:(G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e}),|\cdot|_{T^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})})\to(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
is locally bi–Lipschitz and therefore Lipschitz on
\mathcal{I}^{V,\mathbf{X}}_{0\to T}(\mathcal{K})
, we see that
\mathcal{I}^{V,\mathbf{X}}_{0\to T}:(\mathcal{K},d_{\mathcal{X}})\to(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
is actually
\beta
–Hölder continuous. Thus, we may apply Theorem
3.3
, from which we deduce the following

Corollary 4.13

(Universal Approximation of the Flow of an RDE)
.

Let
\varphi:(G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e}),d_{CC}^{\beta})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an Assouad embedding defined as before and
q=\text{dim}(\mathfrak{g}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e}))
. For every approximation error
\varepsilon>0
and every non-empty compact subset
\mathcal{K}\subseteq G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})

, there is some
m\in\mathbb{N}
large enough such that there are
Z\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times q}
,
\hat{f}\in\mathcal{F}_{\cdot}
,

\hat{t}(\mathbf{y})=\hat{\eta}(\hat{f}\circ\varphi(\mathbf{y}),Z)
for which

\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{K}}|\hat{t}(\mathbf{y})-\mathcal{I}^{V,\mathbf{X}}_{0\to T}(\mathbf{y})|_{T^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})}<\varepsilon.

Although we have applied our result to approximate the flow of rough differential equations, the above universal approximation result actually holds true for any Hölder–like function
f:(G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e}),d_{CC})\to(G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{k}),|\cdot|_{T^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{k})})
for any
e,k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Remark 4.14

(Why not use the Carnot-Carathéodory Metric on
\mathcal{Y}
?)
.

Due to the Ball–Box estimate (see e.g.
[
143
, Proposition 7.49]
), if we equip the target space
\mathcal{Y}=G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})
also with the Carnot-Carathéodory
d_{CC}
, then the flow
\mathcal{I}^{V,\mathbf{X}}_{0\to T}:(G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e}),d_{CC})\to(G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e}),d_{CC})
becomes
1/N
–Hölder continuous on compact
\mathcal{K}\subseteq G^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{e})
, which still fits our framework as our main theorems in Section
3
hold for any Hölder–like function. Therefore one may wonder whether it is possible to use the Carnot-Carathéodory metric
d_{CC}
on
\mathcal{Y}
to apply our main results.

To see why it is more convenient to use the “Euclidean metric on tangent space”
d_{\mathcal{Y}}
note that even the simplest non-commutative Carnot group, namely the Heisenberg group,
G^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
equipped with the Carnot-Carathéodory metric
d_{CC}
, does not meet the criteria to apply Proposition
4.6
since it does not admit a conical geodesic bicombing. A metric space admits a conical geodesic bicombing only if it has trivial Lipschitz homotopy groups; this, however, is not the case for the Heisenberg group
[
150
,
151
]
with its Carnot metric.

A standard
infinite-dimensional
(in the sense of Assouad) example of a quantizable metric space admitting a conical geodesic bicombing are Banach spaces. In the next subsection, we show how regular maps between infinite-dimensional linear geometries can also be approximated within our framework.

4.3
Infinite-Dimensional Linear Geometries

Theorem
3.7
shows us how to generically construct universal approximators between infinite-dimensional Banach spaces
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
with Schauder bases
\{x_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
and
\{y_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
.

Figure 11
:
Summary of the simplest model constructible from approximating non-linear operators between infinite-dimensional Banach spaces
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
with Schauder bases
\{x_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
and
\{y_{m}\}_{m=1}^{\infty}
. First one approximates any input in
\mathcal{X}
by its first few basis coefficients, these are transformed by a function in the universal class
\mathcal{F}
, the function’s outputs are projected onto a simplex and then assembled into a vector in
\mathcal{Y}
lying in the convex hull of
\{\sum_{j=1}^{q}\,z_{q}^{(i)}\,y_{q}\}_{i=1}^{N}
for given parameters
z^{(1)},\dots,z^{(N)}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
.

We will first describe a basic application of that result. We take
\varphi
to be the identity map on
\mathcal{X}
and let
\{T^{(n)}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
be finite-rank operators taken to be the truncation maps sending any
x\in\mathcal{X}
with unique basis representation
x=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\,\beta_{i}^{x}\,x_{i}
to its truncated Schauder basis expansion

T^{(n)}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\beta_{i}^{x}\,x_{i}\Big)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,\beta_{i}^{x}\,x_{i}.

(4.13)

Thus,
\{\mathcal{X},\text{Id}_{\mathcal{X}}\}
trivially satisfies Assumptions
3.6
(i) and (ii).

In
[
80
]
, it is shown that any Banach space
\mathcal{Y}
is barycentric with a unique barycenter map given by Bochner integration
\mathbb{P}\mapsto\int_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\,y\,\mathbb{P}(dy)
. As shown in
[
44
, Example 5.1]
, any such
\mathcal{Y}
is a QAS space with the following structure. For
q\in\mathbb{N}
, the
q^{\text{th}}
quantization map
\mathcal{Q}_{q}:\mathbb{R}^{q}\to\mathcal{Y}
assembles a parameter
z\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
into a linear combination of the first
q
Schauder basis elements in
\mathcal{Y}
by

\mathcal{Q}_{q}(z)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\sum_{i=1}^{q}\,z_{i}\,y_{i}.

A mixing function
\eta
on
\mathcal{Y}
can be defined, similarly to Example
4.7
, by assembling any finite set of points
\tilde{y}_{1},\dots,\tilde{y}_{N}
in
\mathcal{Y}
and any weight
w
in the
N
-simplex
\Delta_{N}
via the convex combination

\eta(w,(\tilde{y}_{j})_{j=1}^{N})\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{N}\,w_{j}\,\tilde{y}_{j}.
Combining the quantization
\mathcal{Q}_{\cdot}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}(\mathcal{Q}_{q})_{q=1}^{\infty}
with the mixing function
\eta
produces a QAS space structure on
\mathcal{Y}
, with quantized mixing function,
\hat{\eta}
, defined for positive integers
N,q
, weights
w
in the
N
-simplex
\Delta_{N}
, and parameters
z^{(1)},\dots,z^{(N)}
in
\mathbb{R}^{q}
, according to

\hat{\eta}(w,(z_{j})_{j=1}^{N})\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\,\sum_{i=1}^{q}w_{j}\,z^{(j)}_{i}\,y_{i}.

(4.14)

Therefore, Assumption
3.6
(iii) is satisfied. The resulting class of function approximators, illustrated in Figure
11
, consists of all non-linear operators
\hat{t}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
with representation

\hat{t}(x)\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{N}\,\sum_{i=1}^{q}\,\Big[P_{\Delta_{N}}\circ\hat{f}\Big((\beta_{k}^{x})_{k=1}^{n}\Big)\Big]_{j}\,z^{(j)}_{i}y_{i},

(4.15)

with
n
and
\beta^{x}_{k}
as in (
4.13
),
N
and
z^{(j)}_{i}
,
j=1,\ldots,N
,
i=1,\ldots,q
as in (
4.14
), and
\hat{f}\in\cup_{c}\,\mathcal{F}_{n,N,c}
for some universal approximator
\mathcal{F}
.

Theorem
3.7
guarantees that the class of maps with representation (
4.15
) can approximate any non-linear Hölder operator from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{Y}
.

Corollary 4.15

(Quantitative Approximation of Hölder Operators)
.

Let
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
be a non-linear Hölder continuous operator between infinite-dimensional Banach spaces
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
with respective Schauder bases
\{x_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
and
\{y_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be a universal approximator.
For any error
\varepsilon>0
and any compact subset
K\subseteq\mathcal{X}
there is a
\hat{t}
with representation (
4.15
) satisfying

\sup_{x\in K}\,\big\|\hat{t}(x)-f(x)\big\|_{\mathcal{Y}}<\varepsilon,

quantitatively, where
\mathcal{Y}
is normed by
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Y}}
.

Remark 4.16

(A “Discretization-Invariant” Neural Operator Paradigm)
.

The results in this subsection results are an instance of deep learning models known as neural operators
[
14
,
15
,
152
,
99
]
. These models approximate maps between function spaces, typically Hilbert spaces. An important requirement is that they be “discretization invariant” in the sense that the model parameters do not depend on the evaluation of the input function at any point. Our framework allows us to construct such “discretization invariant” universal approximators between suitable Banach spaces; an example is illustrated in Figure
11
with a Schauder basis in
C([0,1])
given by wavelets. In this case, the basis “truncation levels” and
\hat{f}
’s weights do not explicitly depend on any input of the function being evaluated. We further note that we recover a variant of the Fourier neural operator (FNO) when
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{Y}=L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
and the Schauder basis is taken to be the usual orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
, where
\mathbb{S}^{1}
denotes the circle with its usual Riemannian metric.

Non-linear operators from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{Y}
of sub-Hölder regularity can be approximated by applying Theorem
3.3
. The resulting model will be of a form similar to (
4.15
). To show this, we note that since
\{y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
is a Schauder basis for the second-countable space
\mathcal{Y}
, there is a countably-infinite dense subset of
\mathcal{Y}
of the form
\{\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}\,z^{(k)}_{i}\,y_{i}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
.
Theorem
3.3
, concerns the set of probability measure-valued functions of the form

\hat{T}(x)\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{N}\,\Big[P_{\Delta_{N}}\circ\hat{f}\big((\beta_{i}^{x})_{i=1}^{n}\big)\Big]_{j}\,\biggl(\sum_{q=1}^{Q}\,u^{(j)}_{q}\delta_{\sum_{s=1}^{N_{j}}\,z^{(j)}_{s}\,y_{s}}\biggr),

(4.16)

where
u^{(1)},\dots,u^{(N)}
belong to the
Q
-simplex
\Delta_{Q}
and each
\sum_{s=1}^{N_{j}}\,z^{(j)}_{s}\,y_{s}
belongs to the countably infinite dense subset
\{\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}\,z^{(k)}_{i}\,y_{i}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
.
A direct application of the result implies that for any
\varepsilon>0
, any continuous non-linear operator
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
, and any compact subset
K\subseteq\mathcal{X}
there is some
\hat{T}
with representation (
4.16
) satisfying the probabilistic approximation guarantee

\sup_{x\in K}\,W_{1}(\delta_{f(x)},\hat{T}(x))<\varepsilon.

(4.17)

Leveraging the unique barycenter map
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{Y}
given by Bochner integration, we may “collapse” the measure-valued map
\hat{T}
to a
\mathcal{Y}
-valued map by post-composition with
\beta
as in (
4.8
). The Bochner integral’s linearity allows us to simplify
44
44

44

In more detail:

\hat{t}(x)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\int_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\,y\,\hat{T}(x)(dy)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\,\Big[P_{\Delta_{N}}\circ\hat{f}\big((\beta_{i}^{x})_{i=1}^{n}\big)\Big]_{j}\,\biggl(\sum_{q=1}^{Q}\,u^{(j)}_{q}\sum_{s=1}^{N_{j}}\,z^{(j)}_{s}\,y_{s}\biggr)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\,\sum_{q=1}^{Q}\,\sum_{s=1}^{N_{j}}\,u^{(j)}_{q}z^{(j)}_{s}\Big[P_{\Delta_{N}}\circ\hat{f}\big((\beta_{i}^{x})_{i=1}^{n}\big)\Big]_{j}\,y_{s}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\,\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{Q}}\,\tilde{z}^{(i)}_{j}\Big[P_{\Delta_{N}}\circ\hat{f}\big((\beta_{i}^{x})_{i=1}^{n}\big)\Big]_{j}\,y_{i},

the expression of
\hat{t}(x)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\int_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\,y\,\hat{T}(x)(dy)
to

\displaystyle\hat{t}(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\,\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{Q}}\,\Big[P_{\Delta_{N}}\circ\hat{f}\big((\beta_{i}^{x})_{i=1}^{n}\big)\Big]_{j}\tilde{z}^{(i)}_{j}\,y_{i},

(4.18)

where
\tilde{Q},N
are positive integers and
\tilde{z}^{(1)},\dots,\tilde{z}^{(N)}
belong to
\mathbb{R}^{\tilde{Q}}
.
Since barycenter map
\beta
is
1
-Lipschitz,
[
80
]
(
4.17
) implies the following qualitative guarantee for maps of the form (
4.18
).

Corollary 4.17

(Qualitative Approximation of Continuous Operators)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{Y}
, and
\mathcal{F}
be as in Corollary
4.15
. For any continuous non-linear operator
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
, any error
\varepsilon>0
, any compact subset
K
of
\mathcal{X}
there is some
\hat{t}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
with representation (
4.18
) satisfying

\sup_{x\in K}\,\|f(x)-\hat{t}(x)\|_{\mathcal{Y}}<\varepsilon.

The architecture used in Corollaries
4.15
and
4.17
was generic for any
\mathcal{X}
with the BAP. However, improved approximation rates and specialized constructions for continuous non-linear operator approximation can be given if more structure is available.
We now describe how one can modify the first part of the model in Figure
11
by modifying the feature map. The first example is motivated by the analysis of inverse problems when measurements lie in an immersed (finite-dimensional) sub-manifold of the infinite-dimensional Banach space
\mathcal{X}
.

(a)

Data on A Finite-Dimensional Manifold:
If data lies on a finite-dimensional closed and connected Riemannian submanifold
\mathcal{X}
of the source Banach space then, a bi-Lipschitz feature map can be constructed by considering the distance of any input to a finite number of “reference points” in
\mathcal{X}
.

(b)

No A-Priori Knowledge of Problem:
If we do not know any structure in the approximation problem, then one can always default to the feature map described in (
4.13
).

Figure 12
:
Our framework can leverage additional structure about a learning problem by customizing the feature map in the first segment in Figure
11
.

4.3.1
Feature Maps When Data Lies on a Smooth Compact Manifold

Consider two Banach spaces
B
and
E
with norms
\|\cdot\|_{B}
and
\|\cdot\|_{E}
and a continuous linear map
F:B\rightarrow E
. A common assumption in computational practice is that data in
B
is contained in a finite-dimensional topological submanifold
\mathcal{Y}
of
B
(see e.g.
[
153
]
). In the context of inverse problems, it was recently shown that this “manifold hypothesis” guarantees Hölder stability of the inverse restricted to
\mathcal{Y}

[
57
]
.

We now show how this fits with our results. Suppose that
\mathcal{Y}
is a topological submanifold of
B
and that it is equipped with a
C^{\infty}
-smooth structure
\{(\varphi_{\gamma},U_{\gamma})\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
. We do not require that
\mathcal{Y}
is a smooth submanifold of
B
. We say that
\mathcal{Y}
is
\beta
-Hölder in
B
if there is a constant
0<c\leq 1
such that

\frac{\|\varphi_{\gamma}(x)-\varphi_{\gamma}(\tilde{x})\|_{2}^{\beta}}{\|x-\tilde{x}\|_{B}}\geq c,

holds for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
and every
x,\tilde{x}\in U_{\gamma}
. The forward operator
F:B\rightarrow E
is said to be
differentiable
on
\mathcal{Y}
if
F\circ\varphi_{\gamma}^{-1}:\varphi_{\gamma}(U_{\gamma})\rightarrow E
is Fréchet differentiable and its Fréchet derivative
\nabla(F\circ\varphi_{\gamma}^{-1})
is continuous for every
\gamma\in\Gamma
. A fortiori, we call
F
an
immersion
if it is differentiable on
\mathcal{Y}
, injective when restricted to
\mathcal{Y}
, and if the differentials
dF_{x}:T_{x}(\mathcal{Y})\rightarrow E
are injective linear maps for every
x\in\mathcal{Y}
.

If
F
is an immersion on a smooth manifold
\mathcal{Y}
which is
\beta
-Hölder in
B
then
[
57
, Theorem 2.2]
implies that
F|_{F(\mathcal{Y})}^{-1}:F(\mathcal{Y})\rightarrow B
exists on
F(\mathcal{Y})
and that the inverse-operator is of
\beta
-Hölder regularity. Moreover, since
F
is an immersion, then
\mathcal{X}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}F(\mathcal{Y})
is compact and by
[
154
, Theorem 3.2]
it is a smooth manifold.

Let us suppose that we know
\mathcal{X}
, but we do not assume that we know
F^{-1}
. Assuming that
\mathcal{Y}
is connected, we have that
\mathcal{X}
is a closed and connected smooth manifold. We may therefore endow it with some complete Riemannian metric
g
so that
(\mathcal{X},g)
is a Riemannian manifold. If we can identify a bi-Lipschitz feature map from
\mathcal{X}
to a suitable feature space, then we may apply Theorem
3.11
to approximate the inverse-operator
F|_{\mathcal{X}}^{-1}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow B
so long as
B
also admits a Schauder basis.
Let us construct such a feature map using a modification of the idea in (
4.2
) originally due to Gromov
[
155
]
and illustrated in Figure
12(a)
.

Featurizing the Source Smooth Manifold

Let
(\mathcal{X},g)
be a closed, connected Riemannian manifold. As usual, we view
(\mathcal{X},d_{g})
as the metric space metrized by the geodesic distance
d_{g}
, defined for any
x,\tilde{x}\in\mathcal{X}
as the infimal length of any piecewise smooth curve
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
joining those two points (that is,
\gamma(0)=x
and
\gamma(1)=\tilde{x}
)

d_{g}(x,\tilde{x})\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\inf_{\gamma}\,\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{g_{\gamma(t)}(\dot{\gamma}(t),\dot{\gamma}(t))}dt.

To describe the (global) feature map we recall the notion of a
systole
of
\mathcal{X}
, denoted by
\operatorname{Sys}(\mathcal{X})
. The systole is defined as the length of the shortest non-contractible loop in
\mathcal{X}
. That is,

\operatorname{Sys}(\mathcal{X})\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\inf\{\operatorname{Length}(\gamma([0,1])):\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{X},\,\gamma(0)=\gamma(1)\mbox{ and }\gamma\mbox{ non-contractible}\}.

We set
\delta\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\frac{\operatorname{Sys}(\mathcal{X})}{11}
and let
\{x_{l}\}_{l=1}^{L}
be any maximal
\delta
-separated subset of
\mathcal{X}
. For every
i,j=1,\dots,L
it holds that
d_{g}(x_{i},x_{j})\geq\delta
whenever
i\neq j
.
As shown in the proof of
[
156
, Theorem 1.1]
, the map
\varphi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{L}
defined by

\varphi(x)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\big(d_{g}(x,x_{i})\big)_{i=1}^{L}

(4.19)

is a bi-Lipschitz embedding of
\mathcal{X}
into
{\mathbb{R}}^{L}
. It follows that
\varphi
is quasi-symmetric (see
[
67
, page 78]
). Hence,
\{(\mathcal{X},\varphi)\}
is a feature decomposition of
(\mathcal{X},d_{g})
satisfying conditions (i) and (ii) in Settings
3.6
and
3.10
.

We often have more detailed information about the source and target metric spaces on which the non-linear operator
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is defined. We now provide an inverse-problem-theoretic example when this additional structure can be leveraged to construct a feature map.

4.3.2
Feature Maps From Inverse Problems

In a typical inverse problem, a parameter fuction varying inside a manifold with a boundary, often a spatially varying coefficient function in a partial differential equation, must be recovered from boundary data. The modulus of continuity of the inverse map may be significantly worse than Lipschitz or Hölder
[
157
,
158
,
159
,
160
,
161
]
. Hölder stability can sometimes be obtained by imposing additional assumptions
[
162
,
163
,
164
]
. Theorem
5.11
applies both to the weakly stable and to Hölder stable inverses, but Theorems
3.7
and
3.11
give sharper, quantitative guarantees when the inverse is Hölder.

We consider an example for a wave equation where the direct map is Lipschitz-stable and determining an unknown coefficient function is Hölder-stable. We will construct a feature map from the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map which corresponds to the measurements on the
boundary of an unknown body.

Let us now formulate rigorously the inverse problem for a wave equation.
Let
M\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
n\geq 2
be a simply connected bounded open set with
C^{\infty}
-smooth boundary,
H^{k,p}(M)
be the Sobolev space of functions having
k
weak derivatives in
L^{p}(M)
, see
[
165
]
and
[
166
, Sec. 4.2]
. We denote
H^{k}(M)=H^{k,2}(M)
,
H^{k,p}_{0}(M)=\hbox{cl}_{H^{k,p}(M)}(C^{\infty}_{0}(M))
, and
H^{k}_{0}(M)=\hbox{cl}_{H^{k}(M)}(C^{\infty}_{0}(M))
.
In particular,
H^{1}_{0}(M)=\{u\in H^{1}(M):\ u|_{{\partial}M}=0\}
and

\|u\|_{H^{r}_{0}(M)}=\|u\|_{H^{r}(M)}
for
u\in H^{r}_{0}(M)
.

Let
g=(g_{jk}(x))_{j,k=1}^{n}
be a
C^{\infty}
-smooth Riemannian metric on
M\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We assume that
(M,g)
is a simple Riemannian manifold,
that is,
M
is simply connected, its boundary is strictly convex, and the geodesics of
(M,g)
have no conjugate points.
Moreover, assume that

|g_{jk}(x)-\delta_{jk}|\leq\varepsilon_{0},\quad\hbox{for $x\in M$, $j,k=1,2,\dots,n$}

(4.20)

and

T>\hbox{diam}(M,g)
.
We let
1<p<\infty
,
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
be large enough,
\varepsilon_{0}>0
be small enough,
R_{0}>0
, and

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}=\bigg\{q:M\to\mathbb{R}\ \,\bigg|\ q\in H^{k+1,p}_{0}(M),\ \|q\|_{H^{k+1,p}(M)}\leq R_{0}\bigg\}.

(4.21)

We define in
\mathcal{X}
the distances by

d_{\mathcal{X}}(q_{1},q_{2})=\|q_{1}-q_{2}\|_{H^{k,p}(M)}.

Lemma 4.18
.

The metric space

(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})
is complete.

Next,
we consider a classical inverse problem for the wave equation, that is the determination of the (lower order) coefficient function
q(x)
from the boundary observations. Later
we will construct a feature map
\varphi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow F_{\varphi}
related to these boundary observations. We begin by considering the wave equation

\displaystyle({\partial}_{t}^{2}-\Delta_{g}+q)u(x,t)=0\quad\hbox{on }(x,t)\in M\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+},

(4.22)

\displaystyle u(x,t)|_{{\partial}M\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}=f(x,t),

\displaystyle u(x,t)|_{t=0}=0,\quad{\partial}_{t}u(x,t)|_{t=0}=0,

where
\Delta_{g}
is the Laplace operator associated to a Riemannian metric
g
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{g}u=\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}|g(x)|^{-1/2}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}x^{j}}\bigg(|g(x)|^{1/2}g^{jk}(x)\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}x^{k}}u(x)\bigg),

where
|g|=\det(g_{jk}),

(g^{jk})=(g_{jk})^{-1}
.
Function
f\in H^{r}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
can be considered as a boundary source.

The Dirichlet-to-Neumann map corresponding to the potential
q
is defined to be the linear operator

\displaystyle\Lambda_{q}f={\partial}_{\nu}u_{q,f}(x,t)\big|_{(x,t)\in{\partial}M\times(0,T)},

where
u_{q,f}
denotes the solution to the wave equation (
4.22
),
\nu
is the unit outward normal to
\partial M
and
{\partial}_{\nu}u=\nu\cdot\nabla u
is the normal derivative of function
u
. The Dirichlet-to-Neumann map models all possible measurements that one can do on the boundary of
M
.
For
q\in\mathcal{X}
,
[
167
, Sec. 2.8]
or
[
168
]
, Theorem 2.45 imply that the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map is a bounded map

\displaystyle\Lambda_{q}:H^{s}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to H^{s-1}({\partial}M\times(0,T)),\quad\hbox{for }1\leq s\leq k-\frac{p}{n},

(4.23)

We next construct the feature map and its associated feature space. When
L(X,Y)
is the the Banach space of
bounded linear maps
X\to Y
, we have
\Lambda_{q}\in L(H^{s}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T)),H^{s-1}({\partial}M\times(0,T)))
. Thus one could consider choosing the feature space be the Banach space of linear maps,

L(H^{s}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T)),H^{s-1}({\partial}M\times(0,T)))
. However,
the complicated structure of this Banach space makes it difficult to study learning theory if this space is used as the feature space. Our aim is to construct a feature map that takes values in a Hilbert space.
To this end, we introduce some auxiliary operators.
Let

\displaystyle A:L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T)),

\displaystyle Au=(I-{\partial}_{t}^{2}-\Delta_{{\partial}M})u,\quad u\in\mathcal{D}(A)=H^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\cap H^{1}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T)),

be the unbounded selfadjoint operator with the domain
\mathcal{D}(A)
.
Here,
\Delta_{{\partial}M}
is the Laplace-Beltrami
operator of
{{\partial}M}
and
{\partial}_{t}^{2}+\Delta_{{\partial}M}:H^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\cap H^{1}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
is the Laplace operator on
{\partial}M\times(0,T)
defined with the initial and final conditions,
f|_{t=0}=0
and
f|_{t=T}=0
.

For an even integer
m\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
m>\frac{n+1}{2}
,
let us consider the quadratic form

\displaystyle Q(u,v)=\langle u,v\rangle_{H^{m}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T))},\quad u,v\in\mathcal{D}(Q)=H^{m}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T)),

(4.24)

where
\langle u,v\rangle_{H^{m}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T))}
is the inner product of
u
and
v
in
H^{m}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
. The quadratic form
Q
defines a positive, closed, unbounded quadratic form in
L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
.
Further, the quadratic form
Q
defines an unbounded selfadjoint operator
N
in
L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
by the Friedrichs extension theorem, see
[
169
, Ch. VI, Theorem 2.1 and section VI.3]
. When the inner product
\langle u,v\rangle_{H^{m}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T))}

is chosen in the suitable way (from the equivalent inner products in
H^{m}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
)
We see that the operator
N

is the elliptic partial differential operator

\displaystyle N:L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T)),

\displaystyle Nu=(I-{\partial}_{t}^{2}-\Delta_{{\partial}M})^{m}u,\quad u\in\mathcal{D}(N),

that is an unbounded selfadjoint operator with the domain

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(N)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H^{2m}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\cap H^{m}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{u\in H^{2m}({\partial}M\times(0,T)):\ u|_{{\partial}M\times\{0\}}=0,\ {\partial}_{t}u|_{{\partial}M\times\{0\}}=0,\dots,{\partial}_{t}^{m}u|_{{\partial}M\times\{0\}}=0,

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464ptu|_{{\partial}M\times\{T\}}=0,\ {\partial}_{t}u|_{{\partial}M\times\{T\}}=0,\dots,{\partial}_{t}^{m}u|_{{\partial}M\times\{T\}}=0\}.

By the Friedrichs second represenation theorem, see
[
169
, Ch. VI, Theorem 2.23]
), the square root
N^{1/2}
of the selfadjoint operator
N
is defined in the domain
\mathcal{D}(N^{1/2})=D(Q)=H^{m}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
.
Note that the inverse operator
N^{-1}:L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to H^{2m}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\cap H^{m}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T))

and its square root
N^{-1/2}:L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to H^{m}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
are bounded linear operators.

By Weyl’s asymptotics of eigenvalues of the Laplace operator
A
(see
[
170
]
),
the eigenvalues of the unbounded self-adjoint operator
A
in
L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
have the asymptotics

\lambda_{j}=c_{T,M}j^{2/(n+1)}(1+o(1)).

As
m>\frac{n+1}{2}
, the eigenvalues of
A^{-m/2}
have the asymptotics

\lambda_{j}^{-m/2}=c_{T,M}j^{\,-\frac{m}{2}\cdot\frac{2}{n+1}}(1+o(1)),

and the sequence
(\lambda_{j}^{-m/2})_{j=1}^{\infty}
is in
\ell^{2}
. Thus, when
m>\frac{n+1}{2}

we have that
A^{-m/2}
is a Hilbert-Schmidt
operator in
L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
.

As
A^{m/2}N^{-1/2}:L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
is bounded and
the Hilbert-Schmidt operators are an operator ideal, the operator

N^{-1/2}=A^{-m/2}A^{m/2}N^{-1/2}:L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
a Hilbert-Schmidt operator.

Let now
2\leq r\leq m
be an integer. Below we consider bounded operators
B\in L(H^{r}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T)),L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T)))
. Then,

BN^{-1/2}\in L(L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T)),L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T)))
is bounded.Thus,
BN^{-1}=BN^{-1/2}N^{-1/2}

is a Hilbert-Schmidt operator in
L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
.
Thus
BN^{-1}
is an integral operator with the integral (Schwartz) kernel
k_{BN^{-1}}\in L^{2}(({\partial}M\times(0,T))\times({\partial}M\times(0,T)))
.

The feature space in this example is

F_{\varphi}=HS(L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T)),L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))),

(4.25)

that is, the space of Hilbert-Schmidt operators
L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T)))
.
We note that
HS(L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T)),L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T)))
can be identified with the Hilbert space
L^{2}(({\partial}M\times(0,T))\times({\partial}M\times(0,T)))

by identifying operators and their Schwartz kernels.

To define the feature map for the inverse problem for the wave equation we consider
the composed map of the Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator
\Lambda_{q}
and the smoothing operator
N^{-1}
,
\Lambda_{q}N^{-1}=\Lambda_{q}\circ N^{-1}:L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
. We define the feature map to be the function

\displaystyle\varphi:\mathcal{X}\to F_{\varphi},\quad\varphi(q)=\Lambda_{q}N^{-1}.

(4.26)

Proposition 4.19

(A Lipschitz Dirichlet-to-Neumann Feature Map with Hölder Inverse)
.

Let

m>\frac{n+1}{2}
be an even integer,
2\leq r\leq m
be an integer, and

k\geq r+\frac{p}{n}
.
The feature space
F_{\varphi}
, defined in (
4.25
), has the BAP and the feature map
\varphi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow F_{\varphi}
,
\varphi(q)=\Lambda_{q}N^{-1}
satisfies

(i)

The map
{\varphi}:\mathcal{X}\to F_{\varphi}
is Lipschitz;

(ii)

Assume that the parameters defining the space
\mathcal{X}
are such that
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
is large enough,
p=2
,
\varepsilon_{0}>0
is small enough and
R_{0}>0
.
Then,
the feature map
\varphi:\mathcal{X}\to F_{\varphi}
is injective and it has a left inverse function which extends
to a Hölder continuous map
\Psi:F_{\varphi}\to\mathcal{X}
.

We conclude our examples with finite-dimensional spaces having complicated topological structures.

4.4
Target Spaces with Combinatorial Geometries: Closed Smooth Manifolds

For completeness, we show how our theory can be used to approximate continuous functions taking values in
\mathcal{Y}
which is a closed smooth submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
45
45

45

More broadly, these derivations apply to any topological manifold admitting a triangulation.
. We endow
\mathcal{Y}
with a metric
d_{\mathcal{Y}}
compatible with its topology. In particular, we construct a
d_{\mathcal{Y}}
such that
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
satisfies Setting
3.6
. This metric will allow us to nearly partition
\mathcal{Y}
in barycentric QAS spaces
\{\mathcal{Y}_{m}\}_{m=1}^{M}
whose number of parts
M
on
\mathcal{Y}
is equal to the number of simplices associated with a certain simplicial complex of
\mathcal{Y}
.

One example is the geometric realization of a manifold’s triangulation. For instance, if
\mathcal{Y}
is not only
C^{2}
but smooth, classical results in geometric and algebraic topology
[
171
]
confirm that
\mathcal{Y}
can be triangulated. In either case, we only require that the target topological manifold
\mathcal{Y}
be compact and that it admit a triangulation
K(\mathcal{Y})
, which we may assume is embedded as a subset of the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}
(
[
172
, Chapter 3, Theorem 9]
), where we set
d\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\dim(\mathcal{Y})
and
k\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}d+1
. By passing to a barycentric subdivision
bK(\mathcal{Y})
of the triangulation
K(\mathcal{Y})
, we may ensure that each pair of distinct simplices
\triangle_{1}
and
\triangle_{2}
in the simplicial complex
bK(\mathcal{Y})
are disjoint up to their boundaries. Consider the geometric realization
|bK(\mathcal{Y})|
of the simplicial complex
bK(\mathcal{Y})
, which we recall is given by the set of finitely supported probability measures
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\,w_{i}\delta_{y_{i}}
on
\mathcal{Y}
such that
y_{1},\dots,y_{k}
are distinct vertices of
bK(\mathcal{Y})
’s underlying set of vertices.

We define a metric on
\mathcal{Y}
using the geometric realization
|bK(\mathcal{Y})|
of the simplicial complex
bK(\mathcal{Y})
as follows. By identifying each subset
\Delta_{[y_{1},\dots,y_{k}]}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\,w_{i}\delta_{y_{i}}:\,w\in\Delta_{k}\}
of
|bK(\mathcal{Y})|
with the corresponding standard simplex
\Delta_{k}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\{w\in[0,1]^{k}:\,\sum_{i=1}^{k}\,w_{i}=1\}
via the map
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\,w_{i}\delta_{y_{i}}\mapsto w
we can safely pullback the Euclidean metric on
\Delta_{k}
to
\Delta_{[y_{1},\dots,y_{k}]}
; that is,

d_{[y_{1},\dots,y_{k}]}\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\,w_{i}\delta_{y_{i}},\sum_{i=1}^{k}\,u_{i}\delta_{y_{i}}\biggr)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\|w-u\|_{2}.

Since each
\Delta_{[y_{1},\dots,y_{k}]}\subseteq|bK(\mathcal{Y})|
is a geodesic space, then we may metrize
|bK(\mathcal{Y})|
via the standard quotient metric obtained by gluing every pair of convex subsets
\Delta_{[y_{1},\dots,y_{k}]}
and
\Delta_{[y_{1}^{\prime},\dots,y_{k}^{\prime}]}
of
|bK(\mathcal{Y})|
by identifying

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\,w_{i}\delta_{y_{i}}\sim\sum_{i=1}^{k}\,u_{i}\delta_{y_{i}^{\prime}}

in the respective subsets
\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\,w_{i}\delta_{y_{i}}:\,w\in\Delta_{k};w_{j_{1}}=\dots=w_{j_{l}}=0\}
and
\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\,u_{i}\delta_{y_{i}^{\prime}}:\,u\in\Delta_{k};u_{j_{1}}=\dots=u_{j_{l}}=0\}
whenever the set
\{y_{1},\dots,y_{k}\}\setminus\{y_{j_{1}},\dots,y_{j_{l}}\}
and
\{y_{1}^{\prime},\dots,y_{k}^{\prime}\}\setminus\{y_{j_{1}}^{\prime},\dots,y_{j_{l}}^{\prime}\}
coincide and
w=u
, possibly up to a permutation of indices; see
[
173
, Theorem 3.1.27]
for a definition of this type of gluing construction. We denote this metric on
|bK(\mathcal{Y})|
by
d_{|bK(\mathcal{Y})|}
and by
[
173
, Corollary 3.1.24]
we note that it makes
\big(|bK(\mathcal{Y})|,d_{|bK(\mathcal{Y})|}\big)
into a geodesic space.
This metrizes the topology on the geometric realization
|bK(\mathcal{Y})|
(see
[
172
, Chapter 3, Theorem 9]
). Since
K(\mathcal{Y})
triangulates
\mathcal{Y}
then, by definition, there is a homeomorphism
h
from
\mathcal{Y}
to
|bK(\mathcal{Y})|
(see
[
172
, page 121]
). Pulling back
d_{|bK(\mathcal{Y})|}
along the homeomorphism
h
via

d_{\mathcal{Y}}(y,\tilde{y})\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}d_{|bK(\mathcal{Y})|}\big(h(y),h(\tilde{y})\big),

properly metrizes
\mathcal{Y}
’s topology. By construction,
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
is isometric to
\big(|bK(\mathcal{Y})|,d_{|bK(\mathcal{Y})|}\big)
, wherefrom we conclude that
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
is also a geodesic space.
Denote the barycenter
\frac{1}{k}\,\sum_{i=1}^{k}\,\delta_{y_{k}}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2d+1})
of each simplex
\triangle_{[y_{1},\dots,y_{k}]}
in
bK(\mathcal{Y})
. For any
0\leq t<1
, define the associated “retracted” set

\triangle_{[y_{1},\dots,y_{k}]}^{t}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\biggl\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\,\Big(t(w_{i}-\frac{1}{k})+\frac{1}{k}\Big)\,\delta_{y_{i}}:\,w\in\Delta_{k}\biggr\}.

Because we have passed to a barycentric subdivision of the triangulation
K(\mathcal{Y})
of
\mathcal{Y}
, any two sets
\triangle_{[y_{1},\dots,y_{k}]}^{t}
and
\triangle_{[y_{1}^{\prime},\dots,y_{k}^{\prime}]}^{t}
,
0\leq t<1
, are disjoint whenever the sets
\{y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
and
\{y_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i=1}^{k}
do not coincide. Setting
\{\mathcal{Y}_{m}\}_{m\leq M}
to be the pre-images
h^{-1}(\triangle_{[y_{1},\dots,y_{k}]})
in
\mathcal{Y}
of the geodesic triangles in
|bK(\mathcal{Y})|
with vertices belonging to the vertex set of
bK(\mathcal{Y})
, the above implies that the collection
\{\mathcal{Y}_{m}\}_{m\leq M}
satisfies Definition
3.8
(i) and (ii).

As in Remark
3.12
, we do not need to define
\eta
globally, but we can instead only define it on each
\mathcal{Y}_{m}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}h^{-1}(\Delta_{[y_{1},\dots,y_{k}]})
, where
M
is the number of such geodesic triangles (where geodesics are given under the glued metric). Here, each
\eta^{m}
is given by

\eta^{m}(w,(u_{n})_{n=1}^{N})\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}h^{-1}\Big(\sum_{n=1}^{N}\,w_{n}h(u_{n})\Big),

where
N\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,
w\in\Delta_{N}
, and
u_{1},\dots,u_{N}\in\mathcal{Y}_{m}
; for
m=1,\dots,M
.
It remains to quantize
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
in order to conclude that
\big(\{\mathcal{Y}_{m}\}_{m\leq M},(Q_{q})_{q\in\mathbb{N}_{+}},\eta^{m}\big)
is a geodesic partition of
\mathcal{Y}
. For every
q\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and set
D_{q}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}d+1
, we define the quantization maps
Q_{q}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}Q_{1}
by

Q_{1}(z_{1},\dots,z_{d+1})\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}h^{-1}\big(\sum_{i=1}^{d+1}\,[P_{\Delta_{d+1}}(z_{1},\dots,z_{d+1})]_{i}\,y_{i}^{(m)}\big),

where
\{y_{i}^{(m)}\}_{i=1}^{d+1}
is the set of vertices defining the
m^{th}
simplex in
|bK(\mathcal{Y})|
.

5
Proof of Main Results

This section is organized as follows. First, we present several technical lemmata in Section
5.1
, on which the other results are built.
Section
5.2
contains the proof of our main qualitative approximation results.
Section
5.3
is devoted to the proof of our quantitative approximation results.

5.1
Technical Lemmata

Lemma 5.1

(Embedding of the
1
-Wasserstein Space into the Lipschitz-Free Space)
.

Fix an arbitrary base-point
y_{0}
in a Polish metric space
\mathcal{Y}
.
Let
\left({\text{\AE}}(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}},y_{0}),\|\cdot\|_{{\text{\AE}}}\right)
denote the Lipschitz-free Banach space over the pointed metric space
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}},y_{0})
; see
[
174
]
or
[
175
]
for a an overview of the topic. Consider the isometric embedding

\displaystyle\Phi:(\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y}),W_{1})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\left({\text{\AE}}(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}},y_{0}),\|\cdot\|_{{\text{\AE}}}\right)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\mapsto\left[g\mapsto\int_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\,g(y)\,\mathcal{P}(dy)-g(y_{0})\right].

\Phi\left(\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})\right)
is a separable, closed and convex subset of
\left({\text{\AE}}(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}},y_{0}),\|\cdot\|_{{\text{\AE}}}\right)
.

Proof of Lemma
5.1
.

By linearity of integration, the image of
(\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y}),W_{1})
under
\Phi
is convex. Since
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
is complete and separable, then so is
(\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y}),W_{1})
; hence,
\Phi\left(\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})\right)
is a separable, closed and convex subset of
\left({\text{\AE}}(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}},y_{0}),\|\cdot\|_{{\text{\AE}}}\right)
.
∎

The following Lemma is a quantitative version of Lemma
2.4
. It is also a variant of
[
67
, Theorem 10.18]
with explicit constants and additionally ensures the generalized snowflake isn’t only a quasisymmetric map but that
d^{\omega}
also defines a genuine metric.

Lemma 5.2

(Generalized Snowflakes are Quasisymmetric to Their Original Space)
.

Let
\omega
be a Hölder-like modulus and
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})
be a metric space. Then,
d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\omega\circ d_{\mathcal{X}}
defines a metric and the map
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})\ni x\mapsto x\in(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega})
is quasisymmetric. Furthermore, if
K\Subset\mathcal{X}
has doubling constant
C_{(K,d_{\mathcal{X}})}
for the metric
d_{\mathcal{X}}
, then
K
has doubling constant

C_{(K,d_{\mathcal{X}})}^{\lceil-\log_{2}(h_{\omega}^{\dagger}(\frac{1}{4}))/4\rceil}

under the snowflaked metric
d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}
.

Proof of Lemma
5.2
.

Because
\omega
was assumed to be sub-additive,
d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}
satisfies the triangle inequality, and since
\omega(0)=0
and
\omega
is injective (since it is strictly increasing),
d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}(x_{1},x_{2})=0=d_{\mathcal{X}}(x_{1},x_{2})
if and only if
x_{1}=x_{2}
. Hence,
d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}
is a metric.
Now, let
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in K
and
t>0
be such that
d_{\mathcal{X}}(x_{1},x_{2})\leq td_{\mathcal{X}}(x_{1},x_{3})
. Since
\omega
is strictly increasing,

d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}(x_{1},x_{2})\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\omega\Big(d_{\mathcal{X}}(x_{1},x_{2})\Big)\leq\omega\Big(t\,d_{\mathcal{X}}(x_{1},x_{3})\Big)\leq h_{\omega}(t)\omega(d_{\mathcal{X}}(x_{1},x_{3}))\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\eta(t)d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}(x_{1},x_{3}),

where
\eta\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}h_{\omega}
. But then the map
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})\ni x\mapsto x\in(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega})
is quasisymmetric with
\eta=h_{\omega}
.

The last claim follows from Lemma
5.12
.
∎

Lemma 5.3

(Extension)
.

Let
K
be a closed doubling subset of a metric space
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})
, with doubling constant
C_{K}>0
, and let
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
be a barycentric Polish metric space. Let
C^{\omega-\text{H\"{o}l}}((K,d_{\mathcal{X}}),(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}}))
denote the space of all
\omega
–Hölder–like functions from
(K,d_{\mathcal{X}})
to
(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
. There exists an extension operator

\mathcal{E}:\,C^{\omega-\text{H\"{o}l}}((K,d_{\mathcal{X}}),(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}}))\rightarrow C\big((\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}}),(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})\big)

such that, for each
f\in C^{\omega-\text{H\"{o}l}}((K,d_{\mathcal{X}}),(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}}))
the map
\mathcal{E}(f)\in C\big((\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}}),(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})\big)
has modulus of continuity (with respect to
d_{\mathcal{X}}
)

\omega_{\mathcal{E}(f)}=\operatorname{Lip}(f,d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega})c\log(C_{K,d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}})\omega(\cdot),

where
C_{K,d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}}=C_{(K,d_{\mathcal{X}})}^{\lceil-\log_{2}(h_{\omega}^{\dagger}(\frac{1}{4}))/4\rceil}>0
is the doubling constant of
K
under
d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}
and
c>0
is an absolute constant.

Proof of Lemma
5.3
.

Since
\mathcal{Y}
is barycentric then, there exists a
1
-Lipschitz barycenter map
\beta_{\mathcal{Y}}:\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
satisfying

\beta_{\mathcal{Y}}(\delta_{y})=y,

for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
.
By Lemma
2.4
, the map
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})\ni x\mapsto x\in(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega})
is quasisymmetric. Since
K
is doubling then by
[
67
, Theorem 10.18]

K
is a doubling subset of
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega})
.
Let
f:(K,d_{\mathcal{X}})\rightarrow(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})
be a (generalized)
\omega
–Hölder function so that it is a Lipschitz function on
(K,d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega})
.

Since
K
is closed,
[
176
, Theorem 3.2]
implies that there exists a random
projection
\Pi\in\operatorname{Lip}((\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}),\mathcal{P}_{1}(K))

(that is,
\Pi_{x}=\delta_{x}
whenever
x\in K
) with Lipschitz constant
\operatorname{Lip}(\Pi)\leq c\log(C_{K,d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}})
, where
c>0
is an absolute constant and
C_{K,d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}}
is the doubling constant of
K
with respect to the (generalized) snowflaked metric
d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}
. (This constant is explicitly computed in Lemma
5.2
). Fix an arbitrary
y_{0}\in\mathcal{Y}
and let
\Phi
be the isometric embedding of Lemma
5.1
.
In a similar spirit to
[
177
, Theorem 2.4]
and
[
176
, Remark 3.3]
, we define the extension map

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}:\operatorname{Lip}((K,d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}),\mathcal{Y})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\operatorname{Lip}((\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}),\mathcal{Y})

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto\left[x\mapsto\beta_{\mathcal{Y}}\Biggl(\Phi|_{\Phi\big(\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})\big)}^{-1}\biggl(\int_{u\in K}\,\Phi\circ\delta_{f(u)}\Pi_{x}(du)\biggr)\Biggr)\right],

where
\int\cdot\Pi_{x}
denotes the Bochner integral on the Lipschitz-free space
(\mbox{{\text{\AE}}}(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}},y_{0}),\|\cdot\|_{\mbox{{\text{\AE}}}})
with respect to the measure
\Pi_{x}
.
First, we note that
\mathcal{E}
is well-defined as
\Phi(\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y}))
is (non-empty) closed and convex by Lemma
5.1
(noting that
\mathcal{Y}
is Polish); hence, for every
x\in\mathcal{X}
, the quantity
\int_{u\in K}\,\Phi\circ\delta_{f(u)}\Pi_{x}(du)
belongs to the image of
\Phi(\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y}))
in
({\text{\AE}}(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}},y_{0}),\|\cdot\|_{{\text{\AE}}})
for some
y_{0}=f(u_{0}),u_{0}\in K
. Therefore, as
\Phi
is injective,
\Phi
is invertible on its image and
\beta_{\mathcal{Y}}
is defined on
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})
.

Next, since
\Pi
is a random projection then, for every
x\in K
, we have
\Pi_{x}=\delta_{x}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(f)(x)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}

\displaystyle\beta_{\mathcal{Y}}\Biggl(\Phi|_{\Phi\big(\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})\big)}^{-1}\biggl(\int_{u\in K}\,\Phi\circ\delta_{f(u)}\Pi_{x}(du)\biggr)\Biggr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{\mathcal{Y}}\Biggl(\Phi|_{\Phi\big(\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})\big)}^{-1}\biggl(\int_{u\in K}\,\Phi\circ\delta_{f(u)}\delta_{x}(du)\biggr)\Biggr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{\mathcal{Y}}\biggl(\Phi|_{\Phi\big(\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})\big)}^{-1}(\Phi\circ\delta_{f(x)})\biggr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(x).

Then, for every
x\in K
we have that
\mathcal{E}(f)(x)=f(x)
.

It remains to examine
\mathcal{E}(f)
’s regularity. For any
x,\tilde{x}\in\mathcal{X}
, we have

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathcal{E}(f)(x),\mathcal{E}(f)(\tilde{x}))\leq

\displaystyle\omega_{\beta}\circ\omega_{\Phi|_{\Phi\big(\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})\big)}^{-1}}\left(\sup_{g\in\operatorname{Lip}(\mathcal{Y}):\|g\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}=1}\int_{u\in K}\,g(f(u))\,(\Pi_{x}-\Pi_{\tilde{x}})(du)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\operatorname{Lip}(f,d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega})W_{1}(\Pi_{x},\Pi_{\tilde{x}})

(5.1)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\operatorname{Lip}(f,d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega})c\log(C_{K,d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}})\big(d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}(x,\tilde{x})\big),

(5.2)

where we have used the fact that
{\text{\AE}}(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}},y_{0})
is the predual of
\operatorname{Lip}_{y_{0}}(\mathcal{Y})
46
46

46

\operatorname{Lip}_{y_{0}}(\mathcal{Y})
is the space of Lipschitz functions on
\mathcal{Y}
which vanish at
y_{0}
.
in the first inequality and the Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality for the Wasserstein metric in the second inequality.
Applying the definition of the
\omega
-snowflake, we find that

\mathcal{E}(f)
has modulus of continuity

\operatorname{Lip}(f,d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega})c\log(C_{K,d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}})\omega(\cdot).

Appealing to Lemma
5.2
, we obtain

C_{K,d_{\mathcal{X}}^{\omega}}=C_{(K,d_{\mathcal{X}})}^{\lceil-\log_{2}(h_{\omega}^{\dagger}(\frac{1}{4}))/4\rceil},

which yields the result.
∎

Using the definition of a feature map, we use Lemma
5.3
to factor any function defined on a compact doubling subset
K
of
(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})
through a feature space
F_{\varphi}
. The commutative diagram in Figure
13
summarizes the next lemma.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(K,d_{\mathcal{X}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F_{\varphi},\|\cdot\|_{F_{\varphi}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\varphi}}
