(H^{-s})^{\prime}=H^{s}_{0}
for
s\geq 0
. To simplify
the notations used in the interpolation theory below, we use also the notation

\displaystyle H^{s}_{*}=\begin{cases}H^{s}_{0},&s\geq 0,\\
(H^{-s})^{\prime},&s<0,\end{cases}\quad\hbox{for }s-\frac{1}{2}\not\in\mathbb{Z}.

(5.60)

By
[
179
, p. 72]
, the adjoint operator of the map
S:H^{1}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to H^{1-1/2-\varepsilon}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
, denoted
S^{*}:H^{-1+1/2+\varepsilon}_{*}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to H^{-1}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
, satisfies

\displaystyle S^{*}f=RSRf,\quad\hbox{for }f\in H^{1}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T)),

(5.61)

where
Rf(x,t)=f(x,T-t)
is the time reversal operator.

Since
the time reversal operator defines isometric maps
R:H_{*}^{s}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to H_{*}^{s}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
and

R:H^{s}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to H^{s}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
for
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
we see using (
5.59
) with
s=1
that the operator

\displaystyle RS^{*}R:H^{-1+1/2+\varepsilon}_{*}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to H^{-1}({\partial}M\times(0,T))

(5.62)

is bounded for
0<\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}
.

As
R^{2}=I
, formula (
5.61
) implies

Sf=RS^{*}Rf
for
f\in H_{0}^{1}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
.
Since
H_{0}^{1}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
is a dense subset of
H_{*}^{-1/2+\varepsilon}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
, we see using (
5.62
) that
S=S_{q_{1},q_{2}}
can be extended in a unique way to a bounded operator

\displaystyle S^{e}_{q_{1},q_{2}}=RS^{*}R:H^{-1/2+\varepsilon}_{*}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to H^{-1}({\partial}M\times(0,T))

(5.63)

which norm is bounded by
C_{2}\|q_{2}-q_{1}\|_{\mathcal{X}}
, that is,
S^{e}_{q_{1},q_{2}}|_{H_{0}^{r}({\partial}M\times(0,T))}=S
.
Observe that as the extension is unique, we have by (
4.23
) that
S^{e}_{q_{1},q_{2}}f=(\Lambda_{q_{2}}-\Lambda_{q_{1}})f
for
f\in{H^{1}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T))}
.

Next, our aim is to show that the feature map
\varphi
is Lipschitz. To this end, we will combined the mapping properties of the differences of Dirichlet-to-Neumann maps,
\Lambda_{q_{2}}-\Lambda_{q_{1}}
, which are determined by the formula
(
4.23
) in the space
H^{r}_{0}
(where one looses one degree of smoothness) and by the formula
(
5.63
) in the space
H^{-1/2+\varepsilon}_{*}
(where one looses
\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}
degrees of smoothness). Below, these two inequalities are combined using interpolation of function spaces.

Let
[X,Y]_{\theta}
denote the interpolation space of Banach spaces
X
and
Y
obtained
by using the complex interpolation methods with parameter
0\leq\theta\leq 1
, see
[
180
]
for the detailed definition and properties of the interpolation spaces.
We recall that if a linear operator
B
defines a bounded operator
B:X_{0}\to X_{1}
and
B:Y_{0}\to Y_{1}
and
for the Banach spaces
X_{0},X_{1},Y_{0}
and
Y_{1}
the interpolation spaces
[X_{0},X_{1}]_{\theta}

and
[Y_{0},Y_{1}]_{\theta}
are defined for
0\leq\theta\leq 1
, then by
[
165
, Sec. 3, Prop. 2.3]
, see also
[
180
]
,
see Sect. 2, formula (6), the linear operator
B:[X_{0},X_{1}]_{\theta}\to[Y_{0},Y_{1}]_{\theta}
is bounded and

\displaystyle\|B\|_{[X_{0},X_{1}]_{\theta}\to[Y_{0},Y_{1}]_{\theta}}\leq C^{\prime}\|B\|_{X_{0}\to Y_{0}}^{1-\theta}\|B\|_{X_{1}\to Y_{1}}^{\theta}

(5.64)

with an interpolation constant
C^{\prime}>0
that depends only on spaces
X_{j}
and
Y_{j}
,
j=1,2
.
Next, we observe that the set
{\partial}M\times(0,T)
is diffeomorphic to an
h
-neighborhood
of the set
{\partial}M
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
when
h>0
is sufficiently small, and thus we can apply interpolation
results concerning bounded subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
to
{\partial}M\times(0,T)
. Next we recall some of these
results.
By
[
165
, Thm. 11.6]
, for
s_{1},s_{2}\geq 0
and
0\leq\theta\leq 1
,

\displaystyle[H^{s_{1}}_{0},H^{s_{2}}_{0}]_{\theta}=H^{(1-\theta)s_{1}+\theta s_{2}}_{0},\quad\hbox{when }(1-\theta)s_{1}+\theta s_{2}\not\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2},

(5.65)

and
by
[
165
, Thm. 12.6]
, for
s_{1},s_{2}\geq 0
,
s_{1}\not\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
,
and
0\leq\theta\leq 1
,

\displaystyle[H^{s_{1}}_{0},(H^{s_{2}})^{\prime}]_{\theta}=H^{\mu}_{0},\quad\hbox{when }\mu=(1-\theta)s_{1}-\theta s_{2}\geq 0,\ \mu\not\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2},

(5.66)

\displaystyle[H^{s_{1}}_{0},(H^{s_{2}})^{\prime}]_{\theta}=(H^{-\mu})^{\prime},\quad\hbox{when }\mu=(1-\theta)s_{1}-\theta s_{2}\leq 0.

(5.67)

In other words, (
5.65
)-(
5.67
) mean that

\displaystyle[H^{s_{1}}_{*},H^{s_{2}}_{*}]_{\theta}=H^{\mu}_{*}\quad\hbox{for $s_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+},$ $s_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}$ and
$\mu=(1-\theta)s_{1}+\theta s_{2}$, when $s_{1},s_{2},\mu\not\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}$.}

(5.68)

By
[
165
, Theorems 9.6 and 12.4]
for
s_{1}>0
,
-\infty<s_{2}<s_{1}
,
s_{2}\not\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
and
0\leq\theta\leq 1
,

\displaystyle[H^{s_{1}},H^{s_{2}}]_{\theta}=H^{(1-\theta)s_{1}+\theta s_{2}},\quad\hbox{when }(1-\theta)s_{1}+\theta\cdot s_{2}\not\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}.

(5.69)

Moreover, by
[
165
, Def. 2.3]
or
[
166
, Sec. 4, Prop. 2.2]
, for
\theta_{1}=\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle[H^{2m}\cap H^{m}_{0},L^{2}]_{\theta_{1}}=[\mathcal{D}(N),L^{2}]_{\theta_{1}}=\mathcal{D}(N^{1/2})=H^{m}_{0}.

(5.70)

Then, using
by the reiteration theorem for the complex interpolation method,
see
[
181
]
, and (
5.65
) and (
5.70
), we obtain for
0\leq s<m
and
0<\theta<1
, such that
(1-\theta)\cdot 2m+\theta\cdot s\leq m
and
(1-\theta)\cdot 2m+\theta\cdot s\not\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
,

\displaystyle[H^{2m}\cap H^{m}_{0},H^{s}_{0}]_{\theta}=H^{(1-\theta)\cdot 2m+\theta\cdot s}_{0}.

(5.71)

Below, we use
C^{\prime}
to denote an interpolation constant which is the maximum
of the interpolation constants for all the above used Sobolev spaces.

Using interpolation of function spaces, see (
5.64
),
(
5.68
), and (
5.69
),
as well as formulas (
5.59
) and (
5.63
) , we see that the operator,
S^{e}_{q_{1},q_{2}}
is a bounded operator

\displaystyle S^{e}_{q_{1},q_{2}}:H^{s+{\varepsilon}}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to H^{s-1/2}({\partial}M\times(0,T)),

for
0<{\varepsilon}<\frac{1}{2},

s\geq 0,

s+{\varepsilon}\leq r,

(5.72)

such that
s-\frac{1}{2},s+\varepsilon\not\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2},

and its norm is bounded by
C^{\prime}C_{2}\|q_{2}-q_{1}\|_{\mathcal{X}}
.
Next, we consider the stability of the solution of the inverse problem.
Assuming that
k>1
is large enough and the metric
g
satisfies (
4.20
) with
\varepsilon_{0}>0
small enough,

[
182
]
yields that
the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map,
\Lambda_{q}
, determines
q
in a Hölder stable way under a priori bounds on
q
that we have assumed above,
that is,

\displaystyle\|q_{1}-q_{2}\|_{C(M)}\leq C_{0}\|\Lambda_{q_{2}}-\Lambda_{q_{1}}\|^{\,\beta_{1}}_{H^{1}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))},\quad\hbox{for }q_{1},q_{2}\in\mathcal{X}

(5.73)

with some
0<\beta_{1}<1
and
C_{0}>0
.
This implies that the map
\varphi:\mathcal{X}\to F_{\varphi}
is one-to-one.

Next, we consider the map,
{\Psi}:{\varphi}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathcal{X}
, that solves the inverse problem,

\displaystyle{\Psi}:\Lambda_{q}N^{-1}\to q.

As

\displaystyle F_{\varphi}=HS(L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T)),L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T)))\subseteq L(L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T)),L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))),

we then have that

\displaystyle\|\Lambda_{q_{2}}-\Lambda_{q_{1}}\|_{H^{2m}\cap H^{m}_{0}\to L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|(\Lambda_{q_{2}}-\Lambda_{q_{1}})N^{-1}\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}

(5.74)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\varphi({q_{2}})-\varphi({q_{1}})\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\varphi({q_{2}})-\varphi({q_{1}})\|_{HS(L^{2},L^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\varphi({q_{2}})-\varphi({q_{1}})\|_{F_{\varphi}}.

As the identical embedding
H^{s}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
,
s>0
is continuous, the interpolation results for function spaces, see
[
180
]
,
and (
5.72
) with
s_{\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{2}+{\varepsilon}
, where
0<{\varepsilon}<\frac{1}{2}
,
and (
5.71
)
imply

\displaystyle\|S^{e}_{q_{1},q_{2}}\|_{H^{1}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\|S^{e}_{q_{1},q_{2}}\|_{H^{s_{\varepsilon}}_{0}\to L^{2}}^{1-\theta_{\varepsilon}}\|S^{e}_{q_{1},q_{2}}\|_{H^{2m}\cap H^{m}_{0}\to L^{2}}^{\theta_{\varepsilon}}

(5.75)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\|S^{e}_{q_{1},q_{2}}\|_{H^{s_{\varepsilon}}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to H^{s_{\varepsilon}-1/2}({\partial}M\times(0,T)))}^{1-\theta_{\varepsilon}}\|S^{e}_{q_{1},q_{2}}\|_{H^{2m}\cap H^{m}_{0}\to L^{2}}^{\theta_{\varepsilon}},

where
C^{\prime}>0
is the interpolation constant and
\theta_{\varepsilon}=(1-s_{\varepsilon})/(2m-s_{\varepsilon})
, so that
(1-\theta_{\varepsilon})s_{\varepsilon}+\theta_{\varepsilon}\cdot 2m=1
.
By (
4.23
),
S^{e}_{q_{1},q_{2}}f=(\Lambda_{q_{2}}-\Lambda_{q_{1}})f
for
f\in H_{0}^{1}({\partial}M\times(0,T))
, see (
4.23
), and thus the above
formulas (
5.74
) and
(
5.75
) imply

\displaystyle\|\Lambda_{q_{2}}-\Lambda_{q_{1}}\|_{H^{1}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))}\leq C_{3}\|\Lambda_{q_{2}}-\Lambda_{q_{1}}\|_{H^{2m}\cap H^{m}_{0}\to L^{2}}^{\theta_{\varepsilon}},

where

C_{3}=C^{\prime}\cdot\sup_{q_{1},q_{2}\in\mathcal{X}}\|S^{e}_{q_{1},q_{2}}\|_{H^{s_{\varepsilon}}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to H^{s_{\varepsilon}-1/2}({\partial}M\times(0,T)))}^{1-\theta_{\varepsilon}}\leq C^{\prime}\cdot(C^{\prime}C_{2}\|q_{2}-q_{1}\|_{\mathcal{X}})^{1-\theta_{\varepsilon}}.

Thus by (
5.74
),

\displaystyle\|\Lambda_{q_{2}}-\Lambda_{q_{1}}\|_{H^{1}_{0}({\partial}M\times(0,T))\to L^{2}({\partial}M\times(0,T))}\leq C_{3}\|\varphi({q_{2}})-\varphi({q_{1}})\|_{F_{\varphi}}^{\theta_{\varepsilon}}.

Then (
5.73
) implies that

\displaystyle\|{\Psi}(\varphi(x_{1}))-{\Psi}(\varphi(x_{2}))\|_{L^{2}(M)}\leq C_{4}\|\varphi(x_{1})-\varphi(x_{2})\|^{\beta_{2}}_{F_{\varphi}},\quad x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathcal{X},

with some
C_{4}>0
and
\beta_{2}\in(0,1)
. By the definition of the space
\mathcal{X}
,
\|{\Psi}(\varphi(x_{1}))-{\Psi}(\varphi(x_{2}))\|_{H^{k+1,p}(M)}\leq 2R_{0}

for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}
.
Thus by using interpolation of Sobolev spaces
W^{k,p}
, see

[
183
, Sec. 4.3.1, Thm. 2]
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\|{\Psi}(\varphi(x_{1}))-{\Psi}(\varphi(x_{2}))\|_{H^{k,p}(M)}\leq C_{5}\|\varphi(x_{1})-\varphi(x_{2})\|_{F_{\varphi}}^{\beta_{3}},\quad x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathcal{X},

with some
C_{5}>0
and
\beta_{3}\in(0,1)
.

As
F_{\varphi}
is a Hilbert space and
p=2
,
the Benyamini-Lindenstrauss theorem,
[
184
, Theorem 1.12]
, see also
[
185
]
and
[
186
]
, implies that
the map
{\Psi}:\varphi(\mathcal{X})\to H^{k,p}(M)
,
defined a priori in the subset
\varphi(\mathcal{X})\subseteq F_{\varphi}
, has
a Hölder-smooth extension to the vector space
F_{\varphi}
.
Moreover, by
[
184
, Theorem 1.12]
, there is a
C_{6}>0
such that the extension
{\Psi}:F_{\varphi}\to H^{k,p}(M)
satisfies

\displaystyle\|{\Psi}(y_{1})-{\Psi}(y_{2})\|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq C_{6}C_{5}\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|^{\beta_{3}}_{F_{\varphi}},\quad y_{1},y_{2}\in F_{\varphi}.

Finally, we remark that as
F_{\varphi}
is a separable Hilbert space, it has a
\lambda
-BAP.
∎

5.5
Supporting Results and Proofs

The following lemma shows that the generalized snowflake transform of a metric space is again a metric space and it shows that the generalized snowflake transform of a doubling metric space is again doubling, quantitatively. The second result is demonstrated by showing that the generalized snowflake transform is a quasi-symmetric map
51
51

51

Thus the second part of our argument is an explicit version of
[
67
, Theorem 10.18]
which shows the quasi-symmetric image of a doubling metric space is doubling.
. In particular, this lemma generalizes
[
44
, Lemma 6.1]
which shows provides exact estimates on the doubling number of the snowflaked doubling metric spaces.
Following
[
187
]
, we define the generalized inverse of an increasing function
\eta:{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
by
\eta^{\dagger}(t)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\inf\{s\in{\mathbb{R}}:\,\eta(s)\geq t\}
, where
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
\varphi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
be a quasisymmetric map and let
K\Subset\mathcal{X}
be doubling with doubling constant
C_{(K,d_{\mathcal{X}})}>0
. Then
\varphi(K)
is doubling with doubling constant at most
C_{(K,d_{\mathcal{X}})}^{\lceil-\log_{2}(\eta^{\dagger}(\frac{1}{4}))/4\rceil}
.

Proof.

Since
\varphi
is a quasisymmetric map, it is a homeomorphism onto its image; thus
\varphi|_{\varphi(K)}^{-1}:\varphi(K)\rightarrow K
is well-defined and uniformly continuous. We denote its modulus of continuity by
\omega_{\varphi|_{\varphi(K)}^{-1}}
. We fix a
y\in\varphi(K)
and
0<r\leq\operatorname{diam}(\varphi(K))
. For every
u\in\operatorname{Ball}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y,r)\cap\varphi(K)
we then have that

d_{\mathcal{X}}(\varphi^{-1}(y),\varphi^{-1}(u))\leq\omega_{\varphi|_{\varphi(K)}^{-1}}(r);

therefore,
\operatorname{diam}(\varphi^{-1}(B_{\mathcal{Y}}(y,r)\cap K))=2\omega_{\varphi|_{\varphi(K)}^{-1}}(r)
. By
[
44
, Lemma 6.1]
, there are
x_{1},\dots,x_{N}\in K
with
C_{(K,d_{\mathcal{X}})}^{\lceil\log_{2}(-\log_{2}(\eta^{\dagger}(1/4)/4)\rceil}
covering
\varphi^{-1}(B_{\mathcal{Y}}(y,r)\cap K)
with
\operatorname{diam}(\varphi(B(x_{i},r)))\leq r\eta(4\eta^{\dagger}(1/4)/4)\leq r/4
by
[
67
, Proposition 10.8]
and
[
187
, Proposition 2.3 (3)]
(using that
\eta
is strictly increasing).
∎

The following argument is due to
John Von Name
. We refer the reader to
[
188
]
for the original argument and for a lengthier discussion on the topic.

Proof of Lemma
2.6
.

Since
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are separable, the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets is a separable topology on
C(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
. Therefore, we may pick a dense countable family
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
of functions in
C(\mathcal{X},(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}}))
, where
I
is a non-empty countable (possibly infinite) indexing set. We define the real positive sequence
\{k_{i}\}_{i\in I}
by

k_{i}\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\frac{1}{2^{i}\,\big(\max_{j=1,\dots,i}\max_{y,\tilde{y}\in f_{i}(\mathcal{X})}\,\max\{d_{\mathcal{Y}}(y,\tilde{y}),1\}\big)}.

If
I
is finite then
k_{i}\cdot\text{diam}(f_{i}(\mathcal{X}))
is bounded and if
I
is infinite then, by construction,
\lim\limits_{i\mapsto\#I}\,k_{i}\cdot\text{diam}(f_{i}(\mathcal{X}))=0
. In either case, the metric

\tilde{d}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,\tilde{x})\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}d(x,\tilde{x})+\sup_{i\in I}\,k_{i}\cdot d_{\mathcal{Y}}(f_{i}(x),f_{i}(\tilde{x}))

is a well-defined metric on
\mathcal{X}
.
Furthermore, by continuity of each
f_{i}
, the metric
d_{\mathcal{X}}
generates the topology on
\mathcal{X}
; consequently,

C((\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}}),(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}}))

=

C((\mathcal{X},\tilde{d}_{\mathcal{X}}),(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}}))
. Moreover, by construction, for each
i\in I
we have
d_{\mathcal{Y}}(f_{i}(x),f_{i}(\tilde{x}))\leq k_{i}^{-1}\cdot\tilde{d}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,\tilde{x})
, for every
x,\tilde{x}\in\mathcal{X}
; hence, each member of the dense countable family
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
is Lipschitz and therefore the set of Lipschitz functions from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{Y}
is dense in
C((\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}}),(\mathcal{Y},d_{\mathcal{Y}}))
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

Fix an arbitrary base point
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
and consider the isometric embedding
\varphi_{1}:(\mathcal{X},d_{\mathcal{X}})\rightarrow\mbox{\AE}(\mathcal{X},x_{0})
given by
\varphi_{1}(x)\mapsto\delta_{x}-\delta_{x_{0}}
with
\text{\AE}(\mathcal{X},x_{0})
being the Lipschitz free space.

Case 1 -
\mathcal{X}
is Finite:

Suppose, first, that
\mathcal{X}
is finite. Since
\text{\AE}(\mathcal{X},x_{0})
is spanned by the finite set
\{\delta_{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}}
, it is a finite-dimensional normed space. As all norms are equivalent on finite-dimensional Banach spaces, there is a linear bi-Lipschitz map
\varphi_{2}:(\text{\AE}(\mathcal{X},x_{0}),\|\cdot\|_{\text{\AE}(\mathcal{X},x_{0})})\rightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{\#\mathcal{X}},\|\cdot\|_{2})
. Since
\varphi_{3}:(\mathbb{R}^{\#\mathcal{X}},\|\cdot\|_{2}):x\mapsto(x_{1},\dots,x_{\#\mathcal{X}},0,0,0,\dots)\in(\ell^{2},\|\cdot\|_{2})
is an isometric embedding,
\varphi\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\varphi_{3}\circ\varphi_{2}\circ\varphi_{1}
leads to the conclusion.

Case 2 -
\mathcal{X}
is Infinite

Now, suppose that
\mathcal{X}
is infinite.
Since
\mathcal{X}
is separable then
\text{\AE}(\mathcal{X},x_{0})
is also separable, simply consider a countable dense subset
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
\mathcal{X}
then observe that the span of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I}
with rational coefficients is dense in
\text{\AE}(\mathcal{X},x_{0})
. Since
\{\delta_{x}\}_{x\in\mathcal{X}}
is a linearly independent set in
\text{\AE}(\mathcal{X},x_{0})
, then
\text{\AE}(\mathcal{X},x_{0})
is infinite-dimensional since
\mathcal{X}
is infinite. Thus, the Anderson-Kadec Theorem (see
[
189
, Theorem 5.1]
) applies; whence there is a homeomorphism
\varphi_{2}:\text{\AE}(\mathcal{X},x_{0})\rightarrow(\ell^{2},\|\cdot\|_{2})
. Taking
\varphi\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\varphi_{2}\circ\varphi_{1}
yields the conclusion.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.9
.

Fix an
x\in\mathcal{X}
. The
1
-Wasserstein distance
\mathcal{W}_{1}(\hat{T}(x),\delta_{f(x)})
is defined as in (
2.4
) by minimizing over all Radon probability measures
\pi
on
\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Y}
with “marginals”, that is, push-forwards by the canonical projections of the Cartesian product
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
onto its two components,
\hat{T}(x)
and
\delta_{f(x)}
, respectively. However, the only such
\pi
is the product measure
\hat{T}(x)\otimes\delta_{f(x)}
(see the proof of
[
190
, Lemma 6.4]
for a standard argument). Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{1}\big(\hat{T}(x),\delta_{f(x)}\big)=

\displaystyle\inf_{\pi}\int_{(y_{1},y_{2})\in\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Y}}\,d_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})\,\pi(d(y_{1},y_{2}))

(5.76)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{(y_{1},y_{2})\in\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Y}}\,d_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{2},y_{1})\,[\hat{T}(x)\otimes\delta_{f(x)}](d(y_{1},y_{2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}}\int_{y_{1}\in\mathcal{Y}}\,d_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{2},y_{1})\,\delta_{f(x)}(dy_{1})\hat{T}(x)(dy_{2})

(5.77)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\,d_{\mathcal{Y}}(y,f(x))\,\hat{T}(x)(dy),

(5.78)

where the infimum in (
5.76
) is taken over all Radon probability measures on
\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Y}
with respective marginals
\hat{T}(x)
and
\delta_{f(x)}
, and (
5.77
) follows from Fubini’s Theorem (see
[
128
, Theorem 1.27]
). Taking the supremum on both sides of (
5.76
) and (
5.78
) yields

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\,\mathcal{W}_{1}\big(\hat{T}(x),\delta_{f(x)}\big)=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\,\int_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\,d_{\mathcal{Y}}(y,f(x))\,\hat{T}(x)(dy)\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim\hat{T}(x)}\big[d_{\mathcal{Y}}(Y,f(x))\big].

∎

Proof of Corollary
2.10
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{N}
be independent random variables on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
with
Y_{n}\sim\hat{T}(x_{n})
for
n=1,\dots,N
, and suppose that

\max_{n=1,\dots,N}\,\mathcal{W}_{1}\big(\hat{T}(x_{n}),f(x_{n})\big)<\varepsilon.

Let
\delta>0
be a constant, to be chosen below. By the Chebyshev–Markov inequality
[
128
, Lemma 4.1]
and by Proposition
2.9
, respectively, the following holds, for each
n=1,\dots,N

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(d_{\mathcal{Y}}(Y_{n},f(x_{n}))\geq\delta\big)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\big[d_{\mathcal{Y}}(Y_{n},f(x_{n}))\big]}{\delta}

(5.79)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim\hat{T}(x_{n})}\big[d_{\mathcal{Y}}(Y,f(x_{n}))\big]}{\delta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{W}_{1}\big(\hat{T}(x_{n}),\delta_{f(x_{n})}\big)}{\delta}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}.

(5.80)

As
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{N}
are independent, the estimate in (
5.79
)-(
5.80
) implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\,\max_{n=1,\dots,N}\,d_{\mathcal{Y}}(Y_{n},f(x_{n}))\leq\delta\biggr)=

\displaystyle\prod_{n=1}^{N}\,\mathbb{P}\biggl(\,d_{\mathcal{Y}}(Y_{n},f(x_{n}))\leq\delta\biggr)\geq\biggl(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta}\biggr)^{N}.

Since
N>0
, setting
\delta\overset{\scalebox{.5}{\mbox{def.}}}{=}\sqrt{\varepsilon}N>0
yields the statement.
∎

6
